EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This Final Technical Report for Award DE-FE0032189 is an overview of the algae-based CO2 utilization
research project carried out by MicroBio Engineering Inc. (MBE) and project partners - the New Mexico
State University (NMSU), California Polytechnic University (Cal Poly, San Luis Obispo), Los Alamos
National Laboratories (LANL), and Heliae Development LLC. (Heliae). The project ran from February 1,
2023 to January 31, 2026.
The present project was carried out by MBE with Cal Poly to operate four 3.4-m2 raceway algae ponds in
San Luis Obispo and with NMSU in Las Cruces, NM each at wastewater treatment sites, with the green
alga Scenedesmus obliquus. In parallel cultivation studies the project demonstrated the cultivation of
microalgae using merchant (bottled) CO2 at both sites and simulated flue gas with 10% CO2 at the Las
Cruces site. The algal biomass produced at the Las Cruces site was used in biofertilizer studies by Heliae.
The primary technical objective of the CRIAS project was to demonstrate a pathway to exceed the DOE’s
Carbon Utilization Efficiency (CUE) target of 50%, aiming for a system-wide CUE of 70% or greater while
achieving high productivity. This was achieved through a synergistic approach that combines pH-tolerant
algal strains with a specialized gas-to-liquid transfer system designed to utilize dilute flue gas from
biogas combustion sources efficiently.
The Techno-Economic Analysis (TEA) provided a comprehensive evaluation of the CRIAS platform for
increased Carbon Utilization Efficiency technology platform, modeled at both a commercial scale of 400
hectares (approximately 1,000 acres) and at a demonstration scale of 40 ha. The analysis uses data
derived from laboratory research, pilot-scale field trials, and detailed process engineering models
developed by the MBE team projected economic viability of producing Scenedesmus obliquus for use as
agricultural biostimulants.
Based on the present LCA, this project transitioned during its development from Technology Readiness
Level (TRL) 4 (component validation in a laboratory environment) to TRL 5 (component and/or process
validation in relevant environment- Beta prototype). The geographic location of the modelled facility
matched one of the test sites (New Mexico, USA), while the temporal boundary was 2025.
Major project results and conclusions from this project are presented in a brief summary below:
1. Novel strains of the green alga Scenedesmus obliquus were created by non-GMO technology (UV-
 Mutagenesis) and adaptive evolution, including successful trait stacking through multiple rounds of
 mutagenesis.
2. Novel strains could be grown in 3.4-m2 raceway algae ponds for >30 days with productivities
 dependent on algal strains and cultivation seasons.
3. A new external gas-to-liquid transfer device was designed, and CO2 utilization studies (using merchant
 CO2 or simulated flue gas) were carried out demonstrating over 70% utilization efficiency, depending
 mainly on the pH of pond operation, with the higher pH reducing outgassing losses. At 0.3 m deep,
 the pilot raceways were not designed for high efficiency CO2 transfer.
4. Whole-Genome Sequencing revealed loss-of-function mutations in a UV-mutagenized novel strain.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                              Page 4 of 191

5. Greenhouse studies and field trials demonstrated increased yields when S. obliquus biomass-derived
 biofertilizer was tested.
6. TEA studies demonstrated favorable economics for biofertilizer use at large scales.
7. LCA studies concluded that microalgae production would reduce GHG emissions compared to the
 production of other commercial biofertilizers.
In conclusion, this project advanced the technology of large-scale, low-cost, microalgae biomass
production by cultivating a green algal species that was improved by non-GMO technology, harvested,
and suited as biofertilizer feedstock. This novel approach provides a promising approach to maximize
CO2 utilization. Future work is required at a larger pilot plant scale to demonstrate the technical and
economic feasibility of this process.

1. INTRODUCTION
This Final Technical Report for Award DE-FE0032189 is a confidential summary of the research carried
out by MicroBio Engineering Inc. (MBE) and project partners - the New Mexico State University (NMSU),
California Polytechnic University (Cal Poly, San Luis Obispo), Los Alamos National Laboratories (LANL),
and Heliae Development LLC. (Heliae).
Microalgae farming has large-scale potential for commercial applications and environmental benefits.
Algae productivity (biomass per land area) can be many-fold greater than conventional crops, greatly
reducing land use. Their short generation time (hours to days) will greatly reduce the time and cost of
producing seed cultures and developing improved cultivars, compared with conventional crop plants.
This Introduction provides a brief overview of the project technical objectives, project tasks, milestones,
and deliverables.
1.1. Project Overview and Goals
The green alga Scenedesmus (Tetradesmus or Acutodesmus) obliquus is a highly versatile model
organism widely recognized for its potential in large-scale biotechnological applications. This species is
particularly valued for its robust resilience, demonstrating the ability to thrive under highly variable
environmental conditions. Consequently, it is an ideal candidate for integrated biorefinery systems,
where it can simultaneously facilitate municipal wastewater remediation while serving as a sustainable
feedstock for biofuels and high-value bioactives. Beyond its metabolic flexibility, S. obliquus is notable
for its ease of cultivation in outdoor mass-culture systems, such as open raceway ponds, ensuring stable,
year-round biomass production. Furthermore, the feasibility of using non-genetically modified organism
(non-GMO) strain development techniques, such as adaptive laboratory evolution and UV mutagenesis,
allows for the rapid generation of high-productivity strains, effectively bypassing the regulatory hurdles
associated with transgenics and enhancing its commercial viability.
The goal of this project was to integrate CO2 and Carbon Utilization Efficiency (CUE) with high
productivity algal cultivation on non-potable reclaimed wastewaters and biogas combustion flue gas CO2
sources, for production of biostimulants for use in regenerative agriculture.
The accomplishments of this project lie in multiple innovations that were realized. The first set of
innovations achieved a high CUE and related CO2UE, exceeding 70%, under conditions that can translate
to large-scale, low-cost, microalgae biomass cultivation. The second set of innovations were for
increased biomass productivities with increased CUE to further reduce costs and improve environmental
benefits. The third innovations area was in production of agricultural bioproducts – crop biostimulants.
All these innovations fed into full Techno-Economic Analysis (TEA) and Life-Cycle Analysis (LCA) studies.
In brief, the green alga Scenedesmus obliquus (S. obliquus) was used to:
 1. Demonstrate innovative technologies for efficient Carbon Dioxide (CO2) gas-to-liquid transfer
 and reduced CO2 losses, to greatly exceed the Carbon Utilization Efficiency (CUE) of 50%; and
 2. Development of novel strains to achieve breakthrough biomass productivities that exceed >20g
 Ash-Free-Dry-Weight (AFDW)/m2-day under pond operating conditions allowing for high CUE.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                             Page 18 of 191

A general flow diagram for the process initially proposed to be carried out at the field sites in CA and NM
is shown in Figure 1. High-speed centrifugal dewatering provided the >10% solids biomass feedstock
desired for the biostimulant production process.

Figure 1: Process flow diagram of the CO2 utilization pilot facility used in this project.

1.3. Project Success Criteria
Success criteria were objective, specific, and measurable, and repeatable data were obtained in the
project in each budget period, with go/no-go decision points, and at project completion. This project
aimed to demonstrate that biomass derived from the green alga S. obliquus could become economically
feasible with high CUE from flue gas CO2. The major quantitative success criteria established for, and
achieved during, this project, were:
1. At the end of Budget Period 1 the Go-No-Go Decision success criterion was demonstrated stability &
 measured CUE for the platform strain in >30-day trial.
2. At the end of Budget Period 2 the success criterion was demonstration of at least one improved
 strain in >30 day outdoor trial with near 70% CUE and biomass productivity of 20 g AFDW/m2-d.

1.4. Task List in Brief
The different objectives were addressed through the seven major tasks that are summarized below and
transcended both budget periods:
Task 1 - Project Management and Planning.
The Recipient shall direct the project per the Project Management Plan, manage the budget, coordinate
activities among the partners, document method and results, etc. to fulfill the contractual obligations of
the project. The subtasks follow:
 Subtask 1.1: Project Management Plan
 Subtask 1.2: Performance Data Table
 Subtask 1.3: Environmental Justice Questionnaire
Task 2 - Minimize CO2 Outgassing During Algae Cultivation.
The Recipient will cultivate the green algal S. obliquus strains UTEX3031, improved MBE501-12, and wild
type UTEX393. Cultivation will be carried out in reclaimed water at elevated pH and alkalinities near the
CO2 air equilibrium (pCO2aqueous - pCO2air). The subtasks follow:
 Subtask 2.1: Laboratory Productivity vs. pH/Alkalinity Studies
 Subtask 2.2: Field Studies with Platform Strains at High pH/Alkalinity
 Subtask 2.3: Outdoor Novel Cultivar/Clone Evaluation
Task 3 - Efficient Flue Gas CO2 Transfer into Ponds.
The Recipient will use CO2 produced at a wastewater treatment plant from the combustion of biogas for
electricity generation to demonstrate high transfer efficiencies from the flue gas into the algal ponds
with an external gas-liquid contact loop. The subtasks follow:
 Subtask 3.1: Pond and Flue Gas Transfer System Set up
 Subtask 3.2: Abiotic CO2 Transfer Experiments
 Subtask 3.3: Flue Gas CO2 Transfer Efficiency in Algae Cultivation
 Subtask 3.4: Carbon Mass Balance, Process Model and CUEs.
 Subtask 3.5: Carbon Mass Balance, CUE Analysis and Process Modeling
Task 4 - Algae Strain Improvement.

1.5. Project Award Period of Performance and Extensions
The Statement of Project Objectives (SOPO) and the Project Management Plan (PMP) laid out a series of
milestones and deliverables. While all were met by the end of the project, a no-cost contract extension
was required and granted by DOE-NETL.
The project was originally contracted for 30 months’ duration (February 1, 2023 to July 31, 2025), but
the analysis of the bioenhancer field trial took longer than expected delaying the TEA/LCA completion.
Although the algae cultivation efforts, using merchant CO2, were successfully completed by April 2025,
the continued lack of a CO2 flue gas supply from the Las Cruces wastewater treatment facility did not
allow meeting the requirement of growth of the algae on flue-gas CO2. Consequently, the last 30-day
cultivation trial used simulated flue gas with 10% CO2.
In summary, despite the lack of timely a CO2 flue gas supply, all project milestones were met, and
deliverables submitted. The Task 8 with Milestone 2.7 was cancelled by the DOE in BP2. The project
quarterly reports provided the detailed experimental results, summarized in this Final Report. In
addition, the milestone and deliverable accomplishments are described below in Table 2.

2.2. Task 2.0 - Minimize Outgassing During Algae Cultivation
2.2.1 Background
This task centers on mitigating CO2 outgassing from algal cultures, which is identified as a critical
performance bottleneck in large-scale photoautotrophic production. The task follows a structured
engineering and experimental roadmap to optimize carbon delivery:
Chemical Optimization - The approach utilizes elevated pH and alkalinity levels to maintain pond
conditions near the CO2 air equilibrium, thereby reducing the driving force for CO2 loss.
Methodological Workflow - The task progressed from initial laboratory-scale bubble column reactor
studies to outdoor validation in 1,000 L raceway pond testbeds at the San Luis Obispo, CA and Las
Cruces, NM facilities.

2.2.2 Objectives
The objectives of Task 2 are the validation of improved strains developed in Task 4.0 through controlled,
semi-continuous open pond cultivation. This task focuses on demonstrating the scalability and
performance of optimized cultivars in outdoor 1 m³ testbeds, specifically addressing the project's
productivity and CUE benchmarks.
The field trials were essential for calculating inorganic and organic carbon mass balances and
establishing the CUE performance baseline for our platform strains (MBE501-12 and UTEX393). This
baseline data is the requisite standard for validating the productivity improvements of novel, ALE-
developed strains in subsequent project milestones

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                            Page 24 of 191

Task Description from SOPO: In this task, the Recipient will cultivate the green algal S. obliquus strains
UTEX3031, improved MBE501-12, and wild type UTEX393. Cultivation will be carried out in reclaimed
water at elevated pH and alkalinities near the CO2 air equilibrium (pCO2aqueous - pCO2air).
CO2 outgassing experiments will be carried out initially with laboratory bubble column reactors to
determine algal productivity (mg AFDW/L-d) as a function of carbonate alkalinity and pH. Productivities,
in mg AFDW/L-day at these pH/alkalinities will be compared. Results will provide a starting point for
experiments on reclaimed wastewaters with the outdoor 1,000 L raceway ponds, using wild type algal
strains. In the 1,000 L raceway ponds CO2 transfer outgassing losses will be measured. Results will
provide the CUE for large-scale cultivation modeling and TEA/LCA studies.
2.2.3. Subtask 2.1. - Laboratory Productivity vs. pH/Alkalinity Studies
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will set up bubble column laboratory reactors for indoor
productivity studies with platform strains. Experiments will be divided into two sets to allow for parallel
operation at different pCO2/pH and alkalinities or light intensities. The Recipient will inoculate with
MBE501-12 and UTEX393 and measure algal densities daily for light saturated productivity (mg AFDW/L-
d) as function of pH and alkalinity.
Cultivation of the green alga Scenedesmus obliquus strains was carried out in laboratory Bubble Columns
and productivity analysis was completed.
First, the two Scenedesmus strains MBE501-12 (background strain was UTEX 3031) and UTEX 393
were cultivated to establish a correlation between Optical Density, measured at a wavelength of 750nm
(OD750) and the algal biomass measured as Total Volatile Suspended Solids (VSS) and Total Suspended
Solids (TSS). VSS serves as an indicator of the organic algal biomass, which can be expressed as Ash-
Free-Dry Weight (AFDW).
Strains MBE501-12 and UTEX 393 were batch cultivated in Bubble Columns in 5meq BG11 medium
and maintained at 25°C, under continuous light at an intensity of 300 µmol/m2/sec (Figure 2). Columns
were inoculated at an OD750 of 0.05 in 800ml of BG11. Daily OD750, pH and temperature
measurements were taken to establish an OD750:VSS correlation. Air supplemented with CO2 was used
for bubbling.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                             Page 25 of 191

Figure 2: Scenedesmus obliquus in triplicate bubble column reactors next to the dense starting column for
each strain. Cultures were grown in batch in BG11 medium and maintained at 25°C, under continuous
light at an intensity of 300 µmol/m2/sec. Left: Strain MBE 501-12; Right: Strain UTEX 393.

The growth curves for both strains based on the Optical Density (OD750) are shown in Figure 3 below.
Both strains had similar growth curves showing the typical lag-phase of one day followed by about one
day of exponential growth, then light limited growth for about one day, and finally growth slowed
reaching the maximum density after about six days.

Figure 3: Average biomass concentration (OD750) for MBE501-12 and UTEX393 for triplicate bubble
columns.

Biomass productivities for both strains from the experiments presented above with Bubbling Columns
that had been provided with CO2 supplemented air were 299±40 mg VSS/L-day for strain UTEX 393 and
261±30 mg VSS/L-day.
Based on the biomass productivity data obtained for VSS and OD750 over time, for each of the strains the
correlation between the two parameters was determined. This was important, because measuring the
OD750 takes only a few minutes whereas determining the VSS takes many hours. Based on previous
experience a linear correlation between the two parameters was expected. Such a linear correlation
between VSS and OD750 was determined for the strain UTEX 393 and for the strain 501-12. In
conclusion, the OD750 parameter could be used to deduce biomass concentrations as VSS.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                            Page 26 of 191

It was noted that the pH of cultures increased during cultivation in the bubbling columns as the algae
grew and consumed more CO2. The pH curve increased with cultivation time and correlated to the growth
curves.
In a following set of experiments cultures were grown without CO2 amendment to monitor the response at
higher pH’s, while replicate cultures had different levels of alkalinity.
Scenedesmus strains UTEX393 and MBE501-12 were cultivated in BG11 media of various alkalinities to
establish baseline growth and productivity curves. Laboratory bubble columns reactors were operated to
determine algal productivity (mg AFDW/L-d) as a function of pH/alkalinity, with the pH controlled by a
CO2-air gas mixer. Experimental strains UTEX393 and MBE501-12 were not run simultaneously,
however the methodology was identical. To initiate the experiment, BG11 media was titrated with 0.2%
sulfuric acid to confirm they were at the correct alkalinity of 1, 2, 5, 10, 20 meq. Three bubble columns,
one with each different media alkalinity were inoculated in sterile 5mEq, 10mEq and 20 mEq BG11at an
optimal OD750 of 0.05 and brought to a total volume of 800ml. Subsequent to the initial 2 day bubble
column grow out, it became apparent that neither of the two strains grew at 20meq alkalinity (data not
shown). Consequently, following experiments omitted the 20mEq media.
Strains were inoculated in triplicate in 1meq, 5meq and 10meq BG11 media. Additionally, there was an
abiotic column for each alkalinity, which had a Neptune pH probe. Throughout the experiment, the
columns were maintained at a constant temperature of 23°C and a continuous (24 hour) light intensity of
300 µmol/m2/sec with only air bubbling (no CO2 amendment). pH was continuously monitored through
the Neptune APEX Fusion system and OD750 measurements were taken daily. Atmospheric air was
bubbled through the columns continuously with no CO2 amendment. Figure 4 shows the Bubble Column
setup used.

Figure 4: Shown is the Bubble Column setup in the laboratory with three sets of cultures at 1meq, 5meq,
and 10meq alkalinity. The numbers above the Bubbling Columns indicate the alkalinity for the column.
The three clear Bubble Columns visible in the center are the abiotic controls.
Atmospheric air was sparged continuously through the columns and the pH was measured in the bubbling
columns including the abiotic control following inoculation of the cultures. Data were automatically
recorded every hour by the Neptune Apex system. The pH equilibrated with air over time in all abiotic
control columns.
Following, the two strains were cultivated in the different media. The results obtained with strain UTEX
393 (Figure 5) are discussed below. In brief, increasing alkalinity from 1meq to 10meq resulted in
reduced productivity of the cultures.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                            Page 27 of 191

Figure 5: Average productivity for strain UTEX393 (mg AFDW/L-day) measured as mg Ash-Free-Dry-
Weight/L-day for bubbles columns with 1meq, 5meq and 10meq sterile BG11 media. Atmospheric air
was bubbled through the columns continuously with no CO2 amendment.

Productivities of strain UTEX 393 were lower in these experiments than in those shown in Figure 3,
because in the previous experiment the algae had been supplemented with CO2. In the absence of
supplemental CO2 the algae have drastically reduced biomass productivity, indicating that some CO2
should be added.

Following the productivity results for strain MBE501-12 are discussed. In brief, in contrast to strain
UTEX 393 there was no significant reduction in biomass productivity observed for strain MBE501-12
when the alkalinity was increased from 1meq to 10meq (Figure 6). However, strain UTEX 3031 had a
lower productivity than strain UTEX 393 when cultivated in medium with 1 meq.

Figure 6: Average productivity for strain MBE501-12 (mg AFDW/L-day) measured as mg Ash-Free-Dry-
Weight/L-day for bubbles columns with 1mEq, 5mEq and 10mEq sterile BG11 media. Atmospheric air
was bubbled through the columns continuously with no CO2 amendment.

In summary, the experiments performed in the laboratory in Bubble Columns provided basic information
for outdoor cultivation. Both strains UTEX393 and 501-12 perform best at alkalinities between 1meq to
5meq indicating that outgassing of supplemental CO2 can be minimized. Consequently, following these
experiments the strains were then cultivated at 5meq alkalinity within a pH range between 8.0 to 9.0.

2.2.4 Subtask 2.2. - Field Studies with Platform Strains at High pH/Alkalinity
Task Description from SOPO: Based on indoor results, experiments will be performed on reclaimed
wastewaters with the outdoor 1,000 L raceway ponds, using wild type algal strains. In the 1,000 L
raceway ponds CO2 transfer outgassing losses will be measured.
Set-up and initial grow-out trials were completed for platform strain UTEX 393 and improved MBE501-
12 in 3.4m2 1,000L raceway ponds in San Luis Obispo, California. Strains were grown in duplicate in
reclaimed wastewater (RWW) in an effort to obtain baseline productivity data. Sampling for Total
Suspended Solids (TSS) and Total Volatile Suspended Solids (VSS) was completed 3x a week with
handheld pH, DO and temperature readings. APEX pH configuration was set to 7.5-8 with the goal of
moving to a higher pH once the ponds settled. Ponds were operated with both strains UTEX 393 and 501-
12 side by side in duplicates. Figure 7 shows the pond setup at the at the Sustainable Utilities Research
and Education research station of the San Luis Obispo, Water Resource Recovery Facility (SLO-WRRF),
California.

replicates of strain 501-12.
Ponds were started in a batch mode and harvested using a basket centrifuge. Biomass was centrifuged and
pellets were frozen and sent to Heliae as biomass was required for TASK 6: Product Testing in
Greenhouse with Crops.
Ponds were started with biomass from indoor panel cultures. Consequently, the application of shade cloth
for 2-3 days was used to mitigate the potential effects of photodamage cells might encounter after the
transition from indoor to outdoor cultivation. Figure 8 shows an example of the use of a shade cloth to
prevent photodamage and allow optimal condition for the algae to acclimate to outdoor conditions.

Abiotic Experiments
At the beginning of the project abiotic experiments were performed to provide information regarding the
baseline pH changes for the reclaimed water used in the cultivation trials. For the abiotic field trial, two
ponds were first filled with reclaimed water in the morning of the first day. Ponds were then allowed to
equilibrate for 24 hours without the paddle wheels on and without air or CO2 sparging. Following the 24
hour period, ponds were sparged to reduce the pH to 7.5, which was the lower pH border during field
cultivation.

Figure 9: Two ponds were filled with reclaimed water for the replicate abiotic experiments.

The reclaimed water used to fill the ponds had an initial pH of 7.7 and Dissolved Oxygen (DO, e.g. O2)
content of 7.6 mg/L. After filling the pond, the pH and DO took several hours to equilibrate and become
stable. During this time the pH rose to 8.2 and DO to 12.4, indicating influx of CO2 and O2 into the ponds.
At a pH of approximately 8.2 the diffusion of CO2 into the reclaimed water was anticipated to equal the
CO2 diffusion out of the pond, e.g. the equilibrium pH, and that is seen in this case.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                               Page 30 of 191

Biotic Experiments
Biomass from ponds was harvested on 10/31/2023 (MBE501-12) and on 10/04/2023 (UTEX393) for
biochemical analysis of both strains. The results indicate that both strains were relatively similar in their
biomass composition regarding lipids, proteins and carbohydrates.

content to about 54 %, and total AFDW solids to 89%, with 11% for other constituents such as Nucleic

Ponds were cultivated during July to November 2023 to obtain baseline data for both platform strains
MBE501-12 and UTEX393. Figure 10 above shows images of ponds that had been inoculated in late
October and pond grab samples in beakers to provide an optical evaluation of the general health of and
density of the cultures. Ponds losing their green coloration is an indication of problems with the culture,
often related to abiotic factors such as nutrient levels or other factors in the water source, or biotic factors
such as the presence of a pathogen.
Experiments were performed at three different pH ranges during cultivation (Figure 11). Pond cultivation
from July to November 2023 was done in the pH range 7.5-8.0, because in past years outdoor cultivation
of S. obliquus strains at the field site in San Luis Obispo, CA was successful in that pH range yielding
biomass productivity information for all annual seasons. Average productivities obtained for strain
MBE501-12 during the previous DOE funded ABY2 project serve as comparison for this project. The
average productivity for MBE501-12 during May to December 2020 was 15.3 g AFDW/m2-day, with
higher daily productivity in summer and lower daily productivity in spring and fall. In particular, the
average monthly productivity for November 2020 was about 9 g AFDW/m2-day.

when the strain was tested outdoors during the DOE funded ABY2 project. Consequently, the strain
MBE501-12 performed as expected. Strain UTEX 393 had similar productivity (Figure 12 below).
When the pH of the ponds was raised to 8.0-8.5 the productivity decreased for both strains to about 6 g
AFDW/m2-day. Raising the pH further to 8.5-9.0 did not change the productivity for strain MBE501-12,
but resulted in a further decline of the productivity of strain UTEX 393 to less than 4 g AFDW/m2-day.

Figure 12: Plotted are average biomass productivities during the experimental period of November 2023
represented in Figure 11.
Overall, for the pH ranges 7.5-8.0 and 8.0-8.5 both strains showed similar biomass productivities. In
contrast, the strains productivities appeared to differ drastically in the pH range of 8.5-9.0. It is not clear
what happened to the UTEX393 ponds, but as is shown in Figure 11, both of the UTEX ponds crashed at
the end of the experiment in mid-November. In contrast, both MBE501-12 ponds continued to grow well
and were then terminated in early December after one month of cultivation.
Carbon Utilization Efficiency (CUE)
Determination of the CUE of algal cultures required measurement of multiple parameters. The CUE was
determined according to the following formula, which takes into account biotic and abiotic carbon in the
ponds:

In addition to the CUE parameter, the CO2 fixation efficiency of the algae, CFE was determined. This
parameter only considers the carbon fixed by the algae and contained within the algae.

Generally, CUE and CFE differ for any non-photosynthetic biomass, a minor factor in most cases. To
determine CUE and the CO2 fixation efficiency baseline data were obtained from the raceway type ponds
containing algae for the following parameters:

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                                 Page 34 of 191

1. pH of and Inorganic Carbon in the cultures - The cultures were kept at indicated pH ranges, either
 7.5-8.0, 8.0 to 8.5, or 8.5-9.0. Cultures were sparged with CO2 when the pH reached the upper
 boundary set to reduce the pH to the lower boundary value. Inorganic carbon (IC) content was
 measured for the culture medium.
 On average the alkalinity of the reclaimed water used for cultivation was in the range of 3-6 mEq. At
 that alkalinity the outgassing/ingassing equilibrium pH is about 8.5-9., thus at pH below 8.5 CO2 is
 released from the ponds. To achieve highest CUE ponds should be operated at a pH of 8.5, or above,
 depending on alkalinity. Ponds were operated at three different pH ranges to determine CUE.
2. Biomass concentration and carbon content of the cultures - To determine how much CO2 the algae
 incorporated into their biomass the total biomass and its carbon content had to be determined. Algal
 biomass is determined based on the ash-free-dry-weight (AFDW). Standard protocols were used to
 determine the AFDW of the algal strains. The ash content of the algal biomass is about 10% of the
 dry weight (Table 4 above). The biomass densities for the ponds is shown above in Figure 11. For the
 carbon content of the algae, initially a conservative estimate of 40% of dry weight was used based on
 literature values. Later measurements of the algae carbon content produced high values around 50%
3. Organic Carbon content of the culture medium - Organic carbon is present in certain amounts in the
 cultivation medium either already coming in with the reclaimed water or secretion of organic
 molecules from algae or bacteria that were present in the ponds. For a complete carbon balance, the
 Organic Carbon (OC) concentration in the medium was determined. No clear trend in pond OC
 content was apparent, ranging from about 10 to 20 mg/L and averaging close to 15 mg/L.
4. Carbon provided to the medium/algae - As CO2 was provided to the cultures in gaseous form through
 spargers in the pond, the volume of the CO2 sparged (e.g. delivered) per unit time was determined. As
 CO2 was only sparged when the pH reached the maximum set value, the time for the resulting pH
 reduction of the culture multiplied by the sparging rate allows calculation of the CO2 delivered to
 each pond.
 5. CO2 provision to the cultures - First, the sparger providing CO2 was placed into a measuring
 cylinder and the volume of CO2 provided to the ponds per time was determined by replacement of
 the liquid in the measuring cylinder.
Figure 11 above showed the timeline and biomass accumulation during the cultivation of both strains
MBE501-12 and UTEX 393 at different pH ranges. Parameters were measured regarding IC and OC in
mg C/L in the medium, biomass as AFDW g/L, Volume of CO2 sparged L/min for each pond, and the
daily sparging time for each pond in min. Overall, four ponds were operated with two ponds run as
replicates for each strain.
CUE was calculated for all four ponds and is presented in the bar graph below in Table 5 below which
shows the CUE values obtained for the three pH range. When cultures were kept in the pH range 7.5-8.0,
CUE was about 38%. At the pH range of 7.5-8.0 a large amount of CO2 was outgassed from the
cultivation medium, where, therefore, the CUE was expected to be lowest.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                            Page 35 of 191

Table 5: Carbon Utilization Efficiency (CUE) and Carbon Fixation Efficiency (CFE) for strain MBE501-
12 in pond #18 expressed on a percent basis.

The air equilibrium for the reclaimed water used in the experiments was at a pH of 8.2. Consequently,
CUE increased with an increase of the pH range to 8.0-8.5 by about 15% to about 52%.
In addition to CUE, the Carbon Fixation Efficiency (CFE) was also determined. In contrast to CUE, the
Carbon Fixation Efficiency only measures carbon that is fixed in the algal biomass (based on afdw
determinations). For all pH ranges investigated, CUE is about 9% higher than the CFE.
To minimize loss of carbon due to outgassing of CO2 from the water, ponds must be operated above the
pH of 8.2. Cultivation of algae at that pH range resulted in reduced productivity, even though CUE was
increased. The goal of this project is to develop novel strains that will have increased productivity to
counter any productivity losses encountered by the platform strains.
2.2.5. Subtask 2.3. - Outdoor Novel Cultivar/Clone Evaluation
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will prepare ponds for outdoor cultivation. Improved strains
developed in Subtasks 4.2 and 4.3 that have been identified as the most promising in productivity in the
laboratory trials will be tested outdoors in eight 1,000 L raceway ponds run in duplicate for three novel
mutants and will be compared to the corresponding baseline platform strain. Based on experience, one
testing pond run will take 2-3 weeks, which will provide baseline data on a novel strain’s stability and
performance. The most promising strain(s) will be tested in Task 3 for >30 day flue gas trials. Biomass
will also be generated to serve as feedstock for the bioenhancer studies in Task 6.2.
Ponds were prepared for trials for platform strain MBE501-12 and improved cultivars/strains in 3.4m2
1,000L raceway ponds in San Luis Obispo, California.
Strain MBE501-12 was used as the platform to compare directly to the performance of novel
cultivars/strains when grown in duplicate in reclaimed wastewater (RWW) in an effort to obtain
productivity data. Sampling for total Suspended Solids (TSS) and Total Volatile Suspended Solids (VSS)
was completed at least twice a week with handheld pH, DO and temperature readings. APEX pH
configuration was set to 8.0-8.5 with the goal of keeping the pH at the air/aqueous CO2 outgassing
equilibrium. Ponds were operated with two strains side by side in duplicates. Figure 13 below shows the
pond setup at the at the Sustainable Utilities Research and Education research station of the San Luis
Obispo, Water Resource Recovery Facility (SLO-WRRF), California.

crashed on 5/23/2024 with pond 20 shortly after this. Weather conditions were often overcast and daytime

Multiple parameters were measured online in real time to evaluate the health and performance of the algal
cultures. As dissolved oxygen (DO) originates from algal cells performing photosynthesis, the level of
dissolved oxygen in the cultivation medium in the ponds is a direct measure of photosynthesis performed
by the algae in the ponds. The more dissolved oxygen there is the more oxygen was produced by
photosynthesis. In brief, oxygen is produced during the day and respiration at night consumes oxygen.
Consequently, one would expect an oscillating day/night pattern for the DO over time.
As an example for the DO levels in algal ponds, Figure 17 shows the DO concentration in two ponds, the
platform MBE501-12 (Pond 19) and the new cultivar ALE01 (Pond 17) (derived from MBE501-12
through ALE). Both, strain MBE501-12 and cultivar ALE01 had similar biomass densities during the
April 26th to May 8th 2024 time period.

Figure 17: Dissolved oxygen (mg/L) measurements for cultivar MBE501-12-ALE01 and platform
MBE501-12 from April 26 to May 8. Automatic measurements are taken every hour through APEX
Fusion. Handheld measurements are taken every sample day to validate APEX readings. Automatic
readings are within 5% of manual readings.

The platform MBE501-12 and the cultivar ALE01 showed very similar patterns of DO in the ponds,
indicating that photosynthesis and respiration are very similar. As photosynthesis and respiration are
influenced by the temperature of the environment, Figure 18 below shows a graph with the pond
temperatures for MBE501-12 and ALE01 for the same time period as the DO in Figure 17.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                              Page 40 of 191

Figure 18: Temperature (°C) measurements for cultivar MBE501-12-ALE01 and platform MBE501-12
from April 26 to May 8. Automatic measurements are taken every hour through APEX Fusion. Handheld
measurements are taken every sample day to validate APEX readings. Automatic readings are within 5%
of manual readings.
Overall, biomass productivity and other performance parameters were similar for the platform MBE501-
12 and the Cultivar ALE01 during the 30 day field trial. However, analysis of the biomass indicated that
there may be differences in the biomass profiles between the two algae.
The biomass profile regarding protein and carbohydrate contents did not reveal any significant difference
between MBE501-12 and the new cultivar. However, there may be a slightly higher amount of lipids
present in the new Cultivar ALE01.

Nucleic Acids and cell wall components.

Pond Trial with Pigment Mutant #PM09 began on 06/15
The MBE501-12 pigment mutant #PM09 (see section on Task 4) was scaled up and tested in ponds
outdoors. Pigment mutant #PM09 resulted from UV mutagenesis of strain MBE501-12 and was identified
at MicroBio Engineering having a strong phenotypic mutant with about 50% reduction in Chlorophyll
content. As pigment mutants with this low chlorophyll-containing phenotype may have advantages in
ponds, duplicate ponds were inoculated with this strain on 6/18/24 to test the productivity (g/m^2/day)
against the platform MBE501-12.
Figure 19 below shows indoor bubbling columns during scale up. Note that the low pigmentation
phenotype is still apparent, even after about one year of indoor cultivation, indicating that phenotypes are
stable for quite some time.

Carbon Utilization Efficiency (CUE)
A first experiment was completed for two days (5th and 6th of September 2024) to determine the CUE for
pigment mutant #9 and for the platform strain. Determination of the CUE of algal cultures required
measurement of multiple parameters. The amount of CO2 sparged was measured through application of
mass flow controllers. The ponds were operated at a pH of 8.0-8.5. The dissolved inorganic carbon
content and the dissolved organic carbon content of the pond water was measured. The dissolved organic
carbon in the ponds was low at about 9-10 mg/L.
The inorganic carbon content of all ponds was very similar. The organic carbon content was determined
based on the ash-free-dry-weight of the biomass taking into account the percent of carbon in the biomass
and the dissolved organic carbon.

2.3. Task 3.0 - Efficient Flue Gas CO2 Transfer into Ponds
2.3.1. Background Task 3.0
Algae require a continuous supply of dissolved inorganic carbon to sustain photosynthesis and biomass
growth, and the rate at which CO2 can be dissolved into the pond water directly limits both cultivation
productivity and the Carbon Utilization Efficiency (CUE) of the process. Conventional gas delivery
methods, such as diffusers and sparging stones, are prone to substantial outgassing, in which a large
fraction of the injected CO2 fails to dissolve and instead escapes to the atmosphere before the algae can
consume it. Furthermore, most algal species are cultivated at optimal growth conditions occurring in the
pH range of 6.5 to 7.5. In that pH range most of the CO2 inserted for growth of the algae is leaving the
pond before it can be picked up by the algal cells.
Efficient transfer of CO2 into open algal cultivation ponds is critical to the economic and environmental
viability of using microalgae to capture and utilize carbon from flue gas streams. This outgassing not only
wastes a costly feedstock and drives up the CO2 supply requirements for a given level of biomass
production, but it also erodes the net carbon benefit that makes algae-based flue gas utilization an
attractive climate mitigation strategy.
By decoupling the CO2 transfer step from the pond itself, the external contactor design can be
characterized and modeled independently of pond-specific hydraulics, enabling its performance to be
scaled to hectare-sized commercial pond designs. Developing and validating a gas-transfer-systems that
maximizes CO2 dissolution while minimizing outgassing was therefore essential to achieving the high
CUE values needed for algal cultivation to be both cost-effective and carbon-negative at commercial
scale.
2.3.2. Objectives for Task 3.0
The goal of the project was to increase the CUE achieved during cultivation of S. obliquus to over 70%.
The objectives of Task 3 were to design, construct, and validate an external gas-liquid contact system
capable of efficiently transferring CO2 from a dilute flue gas stream into algal cultivation ponds while
minimizing outgassing losses, and to benchmark its performance against conventional in-pond diffusers
using carbon mass balances at the Las Cruces, NM field site.
This task compares the CO2 transfer efficiency of the novel external contactor against conventional in-
pond diffusers under both abiotic and algal cultivation conditions, using carbon mass balances to
quantify CUE at the Las Cruces, NM field site. The resulting transfer efficiency and CUE data provide
critical inputs for the process models and techno-economic and life-cycle assessments used to evaluate
large-scale deployment of algal CO2 utilization systems.
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will use CO2 produced at a wastewater treatment plant from
the combustion of biogas for electricity generation to demonstrate high transfer efficiencies from the
flue gas into the algal ponds with an external gas-liquid contact loop. By making this external to the
pond, this separates the CO2 transfer from the pond operations, allowing for modeling of such transfer
units for large-scale pond designs (hectares for a single pond). The same 1,000 L ponds and wild type
algal strains used in Task 2 will be used in this work, carried out at a different location, allowing for
comparison of the results in parallel experiments.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                             Page 62 of 191

2.3.3. Subtask 3.1. - Pond and Flue Gas Transfer System Set up at Las Cruces, NM
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will set up four 1,000 L ponds (MicroBio Engineering Pond
Type RW3.4) at the wastewater treatment plant in Las Cruces, NM (Note: the 1,000 L ponds were
shipped and set up by the lead organization at the project partner location). The flue gas from a 300
KW/h biogas power plant will be supplied to the ponds via a pipeline. A hydrogen sulfide (H2S) scrubber
will be added if deemed necessary. An external U-tube will be designed to supply flue gas to the ponds to
be operated either in a co- or counter-current mode.
The transfer unit will be equipped for timed sampling and data logger. Flue gas will be sampled and
analyzed for Volatile Organic Carbon (VOC) with Summa Cannisters. Analysis will be performed by a
commercial laboratory. Pond surface CO2 outgassing will be occasionally measured by drawing off-gas
from the water surface with a portable exhaust snorkel at a known flow rate into the online
Nondispersive Infrared (NDIR) CO2 sensor. Samples will be taken throughout different locations in the
pond to ensure adequate representation.
2.3.3.1. Pond setup in Las Cruces, NM
Four 1,000L ponds were refurbished by MicroBio Engineering Inc. (MBE) and shipped to Las Cruces,
NM. The ponds were then set up by MBE personnel at the Wastewater Resource Facility in Las Cruces,
NM. The figures below show the pond site and the setup of the ponds.

Figure 39: Overview of the East Mesa Reclamation Plant in Las Cruces, NM. The red circle indicates the
site of the ponds on the grounds of the facility.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.

Figure 40: Photos of the four ponds after installation on the site on a concrete slab from two different

2.3.3.2 Design of the external CO2 transfer device.
To meet the CO2 demand of the 3.4 m2 algal ponds, assuming a maximum productivity of 3 g afdw/m2-
hr, or 18 g CO2/hr-pond (~0.4 moles CO2/hr), or about 1.7 L/min of 10% CO2 flue gas (somewhat
depending on temperature) was required, assuming 100% CO2 transfer efficiency. In the original
proposal, a U-tube configuration was to be tested for CO2 transfer. Upon further investigation at the
startup of the project, this co-current flow design for the U-tube was estimated to require a prohibitively
long contact tube.
An alternative CO2 ‘downflow bubble contactor’, DFBC, based on a cone, was conceptualized and
designed by the MBE team, with simple construction, for independent operation of the four ponds
(Figure 41a). This DFBC design as currently envisioned embodies three tubular sections in series with
three different diameters/water velocities enabling suspension of CO2 bubbles of different diameters
for increased bubble detention times to achieve desired CO2 transfer rates (Figure 41b). The currently
projected overall height of this design is 1.5 m (5 ft). If supported by further analysis, a prototype will be
constructed and tested for simulated flue gas (10% CO2 in air) mass transfer. Note the option of
introducing flue gas either at the top or bottom of the device, and with downflow liquid velocities and
void volumes depending on actual dimensions of the stages, bubble sizes, bubble coalescence, etc.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                                Page 64 of 191

Figure 41: Schematic of the down-flow bubble contactor (DFBC) gas-to-liquid dissolution system (Note:
dimensions and flows are preliminary).
The down-flow bubble contactor (DFBC) gas-to-liquid dissolution system was built to enhance CO2
transfer efficiency. The DFBC system is called a “CO2 saturator”. Downflow water was used as a drag
force against the buoyancy of the gas bubbles. By increasing the diameters of the reactor, CO2 bubble
velocity is reduced, and it will have an increased detention time in the reactor to enhance CO2 transfer
efficiency.
At this stage, first, oxygen gas was used as a surrogate to test the gas transfer efficiency of this
saturator. A diaphragm pump pumps the water from the top of the reactor to achieve the downflow
with a pump rate of 10 gallons per minute. A globe valve was used to control the velocity of the outflow
for pressurizing the reactor. An oxygen cylinder was used to supply oxygen gas into the saturator with
an inlet pressure of 11 psi and a flow rate of about 7 liters per minute. The globe water valve and gas
supply valve need to be carefully controlled to enable the gas to enter the saturator and pressurization
of the reactor. After 5 minutes of operation, the dissolved oxygen level in the outflow of the saturator
reached the upper detection limit of the dissolved oxygen meter, which was 20 mg/L. Figure 42 (right)
shows the sufficient gas transfer in the water, suggesting the successful operation of this reactor.
In addition, a water pump with a flowrate of 7.5 gallons per minute was tested. However, the initial
flowrate was not high enough to bring the gas bubbles downwards.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                            Page 65 of 191

Figure 42: The operation of the gas saturator (Left: without the globe valve, the water was not filled in the
middle and bottom of the reactor; Right: controlling the outflow with the globe valve to enhance the gas-
water contact in the middle and lower section of the reactor to improve gas transfer efficiency).
In January 2024 a second saturator was constructed, and initial tests were completed indoors. Both
saturators were installed and tested at the field site in February 2024 (Figure 43).

Figure 43: The photo shows the pond setup at the field site in Las Cruces, NM. The gas saturators operate
with Pond 1 and Pond 3.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                               Page 66 of 191

Following, two more saturators were fabricated so that all four ponds were equipped with the novel
saturators.
Saturator Design
The winter and summer trials conducted in 2024 tested CO2 utilization rate using 100% CO2. However,
when 10% CO2 with 90% nitrogen was used as simulated flue gas, we observed the accumulated nitrogen
gas in the middle section of the saturator due to the low solubility of nitrogen gas (20 mg/L at 20 °C).

Figure 44: Shown is a section of the saturator accumulating a gas bubble when operating with simulated
flue gas.

A force analysis was performed on the gas bubble and further determined the minimal water flow rate,
which can push the nitrogen out of the saturator. The minimal water flowrate is 21.8-30.9 gallons per
minute, depending on the size of the gas bubble. Water flowrate for the trials in 2024 was 10 gallons per
minute, and no gas accumulation was observed due to the high solubility of pure CO2 (1.45 g/L at 25 °C).
The water recirculation through the gas saturation was increased for the trials in 2025. Moreover, a
trapped gas removal line was added at the middle section of the saturator to release accumulated nitrogen
gas periodically. The CO2 saturator was modified with an electrical valve and programmable switch to
periodically release accumulated N2 from simulated flue gas (Figure 45).

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                            Page 67 of 191

Figure 45: Shown are the modifications of the CO2 saturator for use with flue gas.
A summary of the modifications is provided below:
Automatic Valve: An electronically operated shut-off valve is installed at the liquid outlet of the saturator.
The power supplied to this valve from the line goes from the APEX system to the pump through a
programmable switch. In other words, this valve is powered when only the pump is turned on. When the
valve is turned on, the saturator operates normally with liquid recirculation. When the valve is turned off,
all of the accumulated air in the middle section of the saturator goes out with the water at the trapped gas
removal line at the top. After several seconds, the valve is turned on and operates normally.
Programmable switch: This switch supplies power to the electronically operated shut-off valve. A lab-
scale study was conducted to determine the average time taken to complete the air pocket in the middle,

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                               Page 68 of 191

and it was found that it took around 4 – 5 minutes on average for maximum N2 accumulation in the
middle section. Therefore, the switch is programmed to be turned on for 4 minutes 30 seconds and turned
off for 30 seconds when the saturator operates.
These measures ensure the smooth and efficient operation of the saturators even though most of the input
air is insoluble in water.

2.3.4. Subtask 3.2. - Abiotic CO2 Transfer Experiments
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will determine optimal parameters of the U-Tube transfer
system. One aim is to balance gas bubble upflow with liquid downwelling velocities to vary bubble liquid
contact time. This depends on actual bubble size, which is a function of sparger type, placement, bubble
coalescence, dimensions of the transfer tube and ancillary factors (including any effects of algal cultures
themselves, see Subtask 3.3). These initial experiments will be performed without the algae to determine
transfer rates (KLa measurements) and efficiencies achievable in such a unit as a function of key
parameters.
Experiments in the 1,000 L ponds with the U-Tube transfer system and flue gas CO2 at elevated
pH/alkalinity, will be carried out by the Recipient under conditions informed from Subtask 2.2, but also
compared to flue-gas CO2 delivery optimized for efficiency of transfer into the ponds. These experiments
will confirm the results from Subtask 2.2, which will be initiated before this subtask to provide initial
operating conditions, and with the wild type of the algal strain best suited for achieving comparable
baseline productivities and CO2 outgassing losses.
Two saturators were set up and tested at the field site in February 2024 (See Figure 43 above).

2.3.5 Subtask 3.3. - Flue Gas CO2 Transfer Efficiency in Algae Cultivation
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will scale down-selected isolate(s) from liquid stock cultures
to outdoor ponds following established pipelines. Cultures will be scaled from plates to 20-L carboys.
Before inoculation, samples will be analyzed and processed for biomass composition. Each strain will be
inoculated into replicate outdoor ponds (1,000 L). Operating conditions will be based on standard
optimized protocols from Subtask 2.2, with modifications based on data collected during Subtask 3.2. For
each field campaign, cultures will be maintained semi-continuously for at least 30 days. CO2
concentration in the transfer system will be monitored in real time using an online CO2 analysis system
(see Subtask 2.2 for measurements and data collection).
CO2 outgassing from the pond surface is a critical parameter in assessing the overall efficiency of carbon
utilization within the system. Measuring outgassing is important to determine the rate of CO2 release
from the pond's surface, particularly where carbon is present in the supernatant (non-algal portion). This
helps in calculating the equilibrium concentration of CO2 at the surface of the water, providing insights
into the dynamics of CO2 absorption and release. Accurate measurement of outgassing allows for more
precise control of the system's carbon balance and ensures that the carbon introduced into the system is
effectively utilized by the algae rather than being lost to the atmosphere.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                             Page 69 of 191

In this study, a CO2 USB probe meter (model CM-0039, manufactured by CO2Meter.com) was used to
measure the outgassing from the surface of the raceway ponds. The meter was connected to a real-time
monitoring system via a computer, and the data collection frequency was set to every 5 seconds.
Calibration of the CO2 meter was performed at the start of each measurement by exposing the sensor to
ambient air and adjusting it to a stable reading of 400 ppm, which typically required several minutes.

To capture CO2 outgassing from the pond surface, a 64-quart (61 L) latch box was utilized by removing
the lid of the box. The CO2 meter was attached to the bottom of the box, and a foam insulation pipe was
aligned along the edge of the box to create a sealed environment (Figure 45 below). This setup ensured
that no external air interfered with the measurements, providing an accurate representation of CO2
escaping from the pond. The latch box was placed gently on the water surface, with its edge sealed using
the insulation cap to prevent atmospheric interference. The box was held in place using two PVC pipes,
which were secured to the pond surface with U clamps, ensuring stability during the measurement
process.

The abiotic tests were conducted to determine the background levels of CO2 outgassing in the absence of
biological activity. These tests involved measuring CO2 emissions from ponds that were not inoculated
with algae to establish a baseline for non-algal CO2 outgassing, which provided a control for the
biological experiments. This information was crucial for distinguishing between CO2 emissions due to
algal processes and those occurring naturally or from the water itself.

The CO2 measurements were taken automatically when the pond's saturator was activated, which
occurred when the pH reached 9.0. Data was recorded continuously until the pH dropped to 8.5, at which
point additional readings were taken 20 minutes later to capture any residual outgassing and to identify
the stable gas equilibrium value. These readings were essential for the subsequent calculation of the mass
transfer coefficient (KLa), which quantifies the efficiency of gas exchange between the water and the
atmosphere.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                             Page 70 of 191

Figure 46: Shown is the setup of the pond, the box with CO2 meter, and connection to a computer for
measuring real time CO2 outgassing.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                         Page 71 of 191

The following steps were followed to operate the system:
 1) Adjust the pressure at the CO2 tank to 30 psi using the gas regulator.
 2) Turn on the water pump to fill the saturator.
 3) Open the gas valve and set the pressure gauge to 15 psi.
 4) Adjust the CO2 flow rate to 0.25 mL/min.
 5) The APEX system is programmed to activate the pumps when the pH reaches 8.5 and turn them off
 at 9.0. The CO2 solenoid will switch on 1 minute after the pump starts, allowing trapped CO2 to
 dissolve or leave the saturator from trapped gas exit line.
 6) Ensure that all water and gas inlet and outlet lines are properly open, including the water inlet line,
 gas inlet line, trapped gas exit line, and concentrated CO2 water exit line.

The APEX system played a critical role in real-time monitoring and data logging throughout the
experimental trials. The APEX system continuously recorded and stored data with a frequency of 1
minute, 24 hours a day, 7 days a week, until the end of each trial. This high-resolution data allowed for
detailed analysis of various operational parameters.
The output data collected by the APEX system included essential parameters such as pH, temperature,
and dissolved oxygen (DO) levels in each pond. In addition, the system recorded the precise moments
when the CO2 solenoids were automatically triggered to turn on and off, based on the pH thresholds (on at
pH 9.0 and off at pH 8.5). The APEX system also tracked the operation of the paddlewheel pumps,
including the exact times they were turned on and off. Furthermore, it monitored the wattage and current
drawn by the pumps, providing insights into energy consumption during the trials.
All data was graphically represented, showing each parameter's behavior over time, which facilitated the
identification of trends, fluctuations, and correlations between various factors such as CO2 injection, pH
levels, and algae growth conditions. These graphs were crucial for understanding the system's
performance and for comparison between the saturator and conventional diffuser setups.
The output data was exported in CSV (comma-separated values) file format, allowing for easy processing
and analysis using various data analysis software. This file format ensured compatibility with multiple
platforms and tools, enabling a comprehensive evaluation of the CO2 utilization efficiency and the overall
system performance.
Calculation of Carbon Utilization Efficiency (CUE)
Carbon Utilization Efficiency (CUE) is a critical parameter used to evaluate how effectively the algae in
the raceway ponds assimilate the injected CO2. CUE represents the fraction of carbon supplied to the
system that is ultimately fixed into algal biomass rather than being lost through outgassing or unused by
the culture. The accurate determination of CUE is essential for assessing the performance of the CO2
saturator compared to traditional gas diffusers, as it directly influences the cost-effectiveness and
sustainability of large-scale algae cultivation systems for biofuel or bioproduct generation.
The CUE in this study was calculated based on the amount of CO2 introduced into the system and the
amount of carbon incorporated into the algal biomass during the experimental trials. Two main data sets
were required: the total CO2 supplied to each pond and the carbon content in the harvested algal biomass.
The APEX system, which monitors and controls the ponds, was equipped to track the volume and flow
rate of CO2 delivered to each pond, ensuring precise measurement of the total carbon input.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                               Page 72 of 191

CUE-Winter 2024 Trial with Platform Strains MBE501-12 and UTEX 393
Cultures of UTEX 393 and MBE 501-12 were received initially into the NMSU algal cultivation lab and
transferred to BG-11 media, following the recipe provided by Microbio Engineering. Liquid cultures were
established in duplicate and stored both in an incubator and benchtop. After scaling, cultures were plated
onto MASM-D-0 with vitamins for longer term storage.
Algal cultures were scaled up in January and February 2024 in carboys for inoculation of ponds in
February 2024. Following, four ponds were operated at the field site in Las Cruces, NM. Two ponds
(Pond 2 and Pond 4) used the regular diffusers for CO2 delivery. These two ponds were used as controls.
To allow for side-by-side comparison, Pond 1 and Pond 3 were connected to the novel saturators for
delivery of CO2 into the ponds. For all ponds, CO2 delivery was triggered at a pH of 9.0.
It was determined that the novel saturators deliver CO2 much faster into the ponds than the diffusers. This
result indicated higher transfer efficiency of CO2 into the ponds when the novel design was used.
CUE was determined for the 30-day trial in February/March 2024. The ponds were run at an elevated pH
between 8.5-9.0 to keep the ponds at around the air/aqueous equilibrium allowing for minimal CO2
outgassing. CUE was determined as laid out in the section of Task 2 above. Four ponds were used for
cultivation of algae. Two ponds were equipped with the novel saturator and two ponds had the traditional
diffuser for CO2 delivery. In the first two weeks of the trial run Pond 2 (diffuser) and Pond 3 (saturator)
were used for abiotic experiments.

Table 16 summarizes detailed metrics, including gram carbon in organic algae, gram carbon inorganic,
gram carbon biomass produced, gram carbon added, CUE, and carbon fixation efficiency for the winter
trial. This table provides a comprehensive overview of carbon dynamics and system performance,
facilitating a better understanding of the advantages offered by the saturator over traditional diffusers.

CUE-Summer 2024 Trial with Improved Cultivar ALE1 and Platform 501-12
In the summer trials, with the novel cultivar ALE01 and the platform MBE 501-12 strains, the CO2
saturator continued to demonstrate superior performance. The average CUE recorded was 86.5%, which
was slightly higher than the winter values, likely due to increased algal growth rates under warmer
temperatures and longer daylight hours. In contrast, the diffuser system achieved a CUE of 57%. The
improved efficiency observed in the saturator is primarily due to its design, which allows for greater
control over CO2 dissolution, reducing the escape of unutilized CO2 to the atmosphere. These findings
underscore the saturator's potential to enhance algal biomass production by ensuring a steady and efficient
supply of carbon.
For Pond 1, using a diffuser in the first duration resulted in a CUE of 58.2% and a CFE of 52.5%.
However, when the saturator was implemented in the second half, the CUE rose significantly to 88.1%,
and the CFE increased to 85.5%. These results indicate that the saturator was far more effective in
dissolving and utilizing CO2, leading to improved carbon uptake and conversion into biomass.
Similarly, in Pond 3, the diffuser's performance yielded a CUE of 50.3% and a CFE of 42.1%. Upon
switching to the saturator, the CUE improved drastically to 87.8%, and the CFE climbed to 83.3%. This
considerable increase in efficiency further supports the effectiveness of the saturator in enhancing CO2
dissolution and utilization by the algae.
Pond 4 also exhibited improvements when transitioning from a diffuser to a saturator. Pond 4 achieved a
CUE of 61.6% with the diffuser and a CFE of 57.1%. These values increased to 83.6% and 76.5%,
respectively, when the saturator was used in the second half. Although the improvement was slightly less
pronounced compared to Ponds 1 and 3, the results still underscore the superior performance of the
saturator over the conventional diffuser.

Mass Transfer Coefficient (kLa)

The mass transfer coefficient (kLa) is a critical parameter that reflects the efficiency of CO2 transfer from
the gas phase to the liquid phase, which is vital for effective carbon fixation by algae. Experiments were
conducted to determine kLa values for the CO2 saturator and the conventional diffuser systems.

Mass Transfer Coefficient (KLa): Winter Trial

The results of the winter trial demonstrate a clear performance advantage for the CO2 saturator over the
conventional diffuser. Specifically, the saturator achieved carbon fixation rates of 10.39 hour-1, 9.01
hour-1, and 8.66 hour-1, whereas the diffuser recorded significantly lower rates of 2.34 hour-1, 2.55 hour-
1, and 2.50 hour-1. This comparative data highlights the increased efficiency of the saturator in promoting
CO2 utilization and carbon fixation within the algae. Figure 47 below illustrates these findings visually
representing the carbon fixation performance across different systems and reinforcing the notable
efficiency improvement between the novel saturator and the old diffuser system.

Figure 47: The graph shows the results of mass transfer coefficient values for the winter 2024 trial.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                               Page 75 of 191

Mass Transfer Coefficient (KLa): Summer Trial

The mass transfer coefficient for the summer trial was calculated using data from specific dates in June
for both the diffuser and the saturator systems. For the diffuser, measurements were taken on June 6 (Trial
1, D), June 10 (Trial 2, D), and June 18 (Trial 3, D), yielding values of 2.79 hour-1, 3.15 hour-1, and 2.68
hour-1, respectively. In contrast, the saturator's mass transfer coefficient was assessed on June 17 (Trial-1,
S), June 18 (Trial-2, S), and June 19 (Trial-3, S), with resulting values of 9.85 hour-1, 8.87 hour-1, and
9.76 hour-1. These trials provide a comparative analysis of the mass transfer capabilities of the diffuser
versus the saturator during the summer conditions. Results are illustrated in Figure 48.

Figure 48: The graph shows the results of mass transfer coefficient values for the summer 2024 trial.

CO2 Retaining Efficiency

During the winter and summer trials, several datasets were collected to evaluate the CO2 retaining
efficiency based on the outgassing from pond surface under sunny conditions with direct sunlight, which
facilitated a clear understanding of the system’s performance.

CO2 Retaining Efficiency – Winter Trial
Data collected on Feb 20, March 12, and March 26 were used to analyze the CO2 transfer dynamics. The
results are presented in Figure 49 for the CO2 saturator compared with the conventional diffuser.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                               Page 76 of 191

A correction factor was applied to account for the difference in surface area between the pond and the
collection box, ensuring that the measured data accurately reflected the conditions across the pond’s
surface. CO2 inflow flux was calculated using the readings from a rotameter, which tracked the CO2 being
injected into the system. These readings were then processed using the ideal gas law to convert the
volumetric flow rate into mass flux. Finally, CO2 retaining efficiency was determined by comparing the
inflow and outflow fluxes.

Figure 49: CO2 retaining efficiencies for saturator compared with the diffuser for the winter trial

The results from the winter trials showed a clear distinction between the performance of the CO2 saturator
and the conventional diffuser. For the saturator, the CO2 retaining efficiency was exceptionally high, with
values of 95%, 94%, and 94% recorded on Feb 20 (Trial-1, S), March 12 (Trial-2, S), and March 26
(Trial-3, S), respectively. These results highlight the saturator’s ability to achieve near-complete
dissolution of CO2 in the water, even under varying environmental conditions.

In contrast, the conventional diffuser displayed lower CO2 retaining efficiencies. The measured
efficiencies for the exact dates were 83% (Trial-1, D), 81% (Trial-2, D), and 85% (Trial-3, D),
respectively. The diffuser's lower performance can be attributed to its inability to retain CO2 within the
system long enough for effective absorption by the algae. As a result, a significant portion of the injected
CO2 was lost through off-gassing, reducing the overall efficiency of the process.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                               Page 77 of 191

CO2 Retaining Efficiency – Summer Trial

The CO2 retaining efficiency for the summer trials was evaluated for both the CO2 saturator and the
conventional diffuser. The results indicate that the saturator maintained high efficiency across all trials,
achieving 98% on June 17 (Trial-1, S), 96% on June 18 (Trial-2, S), and 98% on June 19 (Trial-3, S). In
contrast, the conventional diffuser showed lower transfer efficiencies, with values of 88% on June 6
(Trial-1, D), 86% on June 10 (Trial-2, D), and 88% on June 12 (Trial-2, D). The results are illustrated in
Figure 50 for the saturator compared with the diffuser for the summer trial.

for the summer trial.

2.3.6. Subtask 3.4. – Carbon Mass Balance, Process Model and CUEs
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will develop a process model, first for CO2 Utilization
Efficiency of the U-tube system as a function of circulation rate, exhaust gas flow rate and geometry of
the U-tube. This will be applied together with cultivation data from Subtasks 3.3 and 2.2 to project actual
CUE at larger-scales. The aims of this process model are to i) optimize the U-tube system with minimal
experimental efforts; ii) aid in mass balance and determination of CUE; and iii) integrate with an algal
growth model for process scale-up and apply to the TEA studies (Task 7).
Outdoor cultivation of both platform strains UTEX 393 and MBE501-12 began on February 12, 2024 at
the field site in Las Cruces, NM. All ponds were operated without interruption from February 12,

2.3.7. Subtask 3.5. - Carbon Mass Balance, CUE analysis and Process Modeling
Outdoor cultivation will resume in ponds with the first identified improved strains. Experimental designs
will be based on BP1 results. For each field campaign, the Recipient will maintain cultures semi-
continuously for at least 30 days. CO2 concentration in the transfer system will be monitored in real time
using an online CO2 analysis system (see Subtask 2.2 for measurements and data collection). The data
will be used to calculate the mass balances, perform CUE Analysis and complete the process modeling
that was begun in BP1.
Summer Field Trial with the Cultivar ALE01 in June/July 2024
The cultivar ALE01 was obtained from MBE for scale-up and testing in outdoor ponds at the testbed in
Las Cruces, NM. On 06/04/2024, the field experiment had been set up at Jacob A. Hands Wastewater
Treatment Facility in Las Cruces, NM. The ponds were inoculated in June 2024 with photos of the ponds
shown in Figure 55 below. Cultures were maintained for a month-long campaign (>30 days) between

strains, platform MBE501-12 and the novel Cultivar ALE01. Biomass productivities are shown for the

replenished with growth medium. Biomass productivity for the period from 06/14 to 06/20 is shown in
orange bars.

In brief, our ALE process yielded a novel cultivar that could outperform the platform strain.

Pond 4 experienced operational issues (leak on 06/24/2024) and failure of the paddlewheel, such that it
experienced less mixing than the other ponds. Metrics of pond health and biomass were collected Monday
through Friday. Metrics included optical density (OD, 750 nm), chlorophyll fluorescence, Fv/Fm, and ash
free dry weight (AFDW, collected pre- and post-harvest). Pond parameters of pH, temperature, depth, and
dissolved oxygen were also recorded. Samples were collected weekly, flash frozen, and retained for
archive. Nitrate and phosphate values were determined by both Biology and Engineering labs at least
once a week to inform feeding. Ponds were harvested when at least one pond reached the harvest trigger
of 0.5 optical density.

Pests were observed in all four ponds; however, their densities remained consistent across treatments.
Rotifers and protozoa (Vorticella) were first observed in P1 on 06/19/2024 and flagellates on 06/28/2024.
Ciliates were seen in P1, 2, and 3 on 07/01/2024. Bacteria were always present. Subsamples from each
pond were used once a week to conduct a bleach plate resistance experiment. The experiments informed
bleach concentrations safe to use on the ponds. A 10 ppm treatment of bleach was applied on 06/18/2024
and a 15 ppm treatment was used on 06/29/2024.

The full experiment is visualized in Figure 60 below with photos of ponds.

In summary, the platform strain MBE501-12 and the cultivar ALE001 performed well in the first two
weeks of cultivation. However, then the Aphelids decimated the algae in the ponds and ultimately led to
culture crashes towards the end of the 30-day cultivation period performed in summer. Consequently,
biomass productivities for the algae could only be determined for the first two weeks of the 30-day
cultivation trial.

In brief, completion of the pond test run with the improved cultivar ALE1 completed Milestone 2.4 ahead
of schedule in July 2024.
