2.4 Task 4.0 - Algae Strain Improvement
2.4.1 Task 4.0 Background
The experience in the algae field is that, although algae naturally are highly productive, economics
require improvements. Higher productivity is essential and high value strains need to be developed from
promising existing strains, ideally from species which have been characterized in some detail for
potential industrial use. Improvements may be achieved through various selection and evolution
methods. Genetic engineering of algae has been slow to yield results and will require regulatory
approval.
As an alternative non-GMO strain improvement has emerged to rapidly deploy novel strains directly in
the field. Task 2 sought to create novel strains from the existing species S. obliquus. Two strains from the
platform species were chosen because they previously demonstrated robust performance in outdoor
open raceway-style ponds.
2.4.2 Objective
The objective of strain improvement by Adaptive Laboratory Evolution (ALE) was to generate novel S.
obliquus cultivars that achieve breakthrough biomass productivities (>20g Ash-Free-Dry-Weight/m2-day)
and high CUE. This is accomplished by selecting for phenotypes that thrive in the specific operating
conditions required for high CUE.
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will carry out Adaptive Laboratory Evolution (ALE)
protocols. In brief, improved strains will be developed by mutagenesis and selection for phenotypes
tolerating elevated pH, with corresponding alkalinity, near the CO2 air equilibrium and with resistance to
oxidative stress, and reduced pigments. Strain improvements will be initially characterized in bench-scale
photobioreactors and improved lines (cultivars, later isolated clones) will be scaled up and validated in
the 1,000 L ponds in Task 2, BP2. The Recipient will set up the Automated Algae Cultivator (AAC) system
and use it for selection of novel mutant populations (‘cultivars’) by rigorously washing out slow growing/
low productivity cells at the set dilution rate and various conditions, allowing selection for multiple
phenotypes in parallel.
2.4.3. Subtask 4.1. - Startup for ALE Process
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will establish foundational multi-step protocols for strain
improvement, including lethality (‘kill’) curves for mutagens and chemicals that generate Reactive
Oxygen Species (ROS), for the two platform strains. The Ultra-Violet (UV) mutagenesis strategy will be
followed.
Completed initial cultivation of Scenedesmus strains in shaker flasks. Growth curves for UTEX3031
(=DOE0152Z), strain UTEX3031 clone MBE501-12 (=MBE501-12), and strain UTEX393 were
completed with the primary objective of comparing growth rates by means of Optical Density (OD750)
measurements.
2.4.3.1. Initial Cultivation of Scenedesmus Strains
Scenedesmus strains UTEX3031 (=DOE0152Z), strain UTEX3031 clone MBE501-12 (=MBE501-12),
and strain UTEX393 were revived from stock cultures in shaker flasks. Following initial grow out, each
strain was cultivated in duplicate flasks on a shaker table under a continuous light intensity of
approximately 80 µmol/m2/s. Daily OD750 measurements were taken for two weeks.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                              Page 94 of 191

2.4.3.2. Lethality Curves for UV exposure
In preparation for the mutagenesis and selection effort, UV lethality curves based on exposure time were
determined for the haploid UTEX393 and the diploid MBE501-12 strains. Strains were diluted with
sterile BG11 media to achieve an approximate OD of 0.25. For each time interval, two sterile 95mm x
15mm polystyrene petri dishes containing 20 mL of culture were placed into the center the Bio Rad GS
Gene Linker UV Chamber without lids. The UV crosslinker was warmed up for 60 seconds before
mutagenizing algal strains. Mutagenized algae were poured into 50mL sterile falcon tubes and kept in the
dark for 24 hours to prevent photoreactivation. Hemocytometer cell counts were completed for 0, 15 and
60 seconds to assess the average cells/mL. Using sterile technique, mutagenized algae were diluted and
plated to a target density of ~1000 cells/plate. MBE501-12 and UTEX393 were plated on triplicate
BG11+ 1.5% Difco Bacto agar plates containing antibiotics and antimycotics. Plates were sealed and
placed under lights with intensity reduced by paper towels for several days before removing the towels to
full-light conditions. After 14 days of growth, average colony counts from triplicate plates were used to
determine UV lethality for each strain which is represented in the Figures 64 and 65 below.

Figure 65: UV exposure lethality graph for the diploid MBE501-12 strain based on average colony counts
from triplicate plates at each UV exposure.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                           Page 95 of 191

Figure 66: UV exposure lethality graph for the haploid UTEX393 strain based on average colony counts
from triplicate plates at each UV exposure.

In summary, strain MBE501-12 exhibited a 50% reduction in viability following at least 20 seconds of
UV exposure, while strain UTEX 393 was more sensitive and experienced significant reduction in
viability at half of that exposure (10 seconds) (Figures 105 and 106). This outcome was anticipated
because strain MBE501-12 is diploid and therefore more resilient to the deleterious effects of UV
mutagenesis due to gene redundancy, while the haploid UTEX 393 strain is not able to compensate for
mutagenized genes resulting in deleterious effects on survival.
In summary, the foundational UV mutagenesis protocol for strain improvement, including lethality (‘kill’)
curves, was established for the following tasks.
2.4.3.3. Selection trials with ROS exposure
Trials with platform strains UTEX393 and MBE501-12 were completed regarding sensitivity to Reactive
Oxygen Species (ROS). The ROS producing chemicals Rose Bengal and Hydrogen Peroxide were used.
Selected concentrations for Rose Bengal Disodium Salt (Fisher Science CAS 632-69-9) and Hydrogen
Peroxide (CVS 3%) were made in sterile 50mL falcon tubes with DI water. Strains were inoculated with
four replicates in 24-well plates with 2mL of 0.05 OD750 inoculum and 1mL of ROS chemical. Plates
were exposed to a light intensity of 72.8 µmol/m2/sec for 8 days. OD750 measurements were taken at the
end of the experiment in order to quantify growth differences.
Results for treatment with hydrogen peroxide of strain MBE501-12 and UTEX 393 are shown below in
Figure 67. In summary, increasing the concentrations of Hydrogen Peroxide resulted in drastic reduction
of growth in both strains.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                           Page 96 of 191

Figure 67: Optical Density (OD750) Hydrogen Peroxide correlation graph for UTEX393 and MBE501-
12. OD750 measurements were taken individually for each well, the graph depicts the average of all 4
replicate wells.

The concentration of 0.008% hydrogen peroxide will be used in future ALE experiments to select for
novel ROS resistant algal strains.
Testing with Rose Bengal also demonstrated the sensitivities of both strains to this ROS producing
chemical. Figures 67 shows the response of strains MBE501-12 and UTEX 393 to this chemical,
respectively.

Figure 68: Optical Density (OD750) Rose Bengal correlation graph for UTEX 393 and MBE501-12.
OD750 measurements were taken individually for each well, the graph depicts the average of all 4
replicate wells.
In summary, our results demonstrated that the growth response of both strains to Rose Bengal (Figure 68)
was not as drastic as the response to hydrogen peroxide (Figure 67). Consequently, the first ALE
experiments will use Hydrogen Peroxide rather than Rose Bengal.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                           Page 97 of 191

2.4.4. Subtask 4.2. - Novel Strain Development Using ALE Technology
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will use methods developed in earlier work and Subtask 4.1
for strain selection and enhanced strains will be advanced by applying an Adaptive Laboratory Evolution
(ALE) procedure. ALE consist of a three-step procedure that includes mutagenesis, selection and
screening. 1) Mutagenesis and recovery. 2) Selection for higher growth rate at elevated pH and
alkalinity, oxygen tolerance, and antenna mutants. All specific selections will be concomitantly under
selection pressure due to washout. 3) Fluorescence Aided Cell Sorting (FACS) (or plating) to isolate novel
varieties, followed by screening for higher productivities.
The Recipient will mutagenize S. obliquus strains and mutagenized populations will be exposed to one of
three selective conditions in the AACs:
• Cultivation under elevated pH and alkalinity allowing selection for low CO2 tolerant strains.
• Exposure to ROS-generating chemicals and select for growth at high oxygen (O2) and low CO2.
• High irradiance for reduced pigment phenotypes with high productivity at solar intensities.
For selection of ROS resistance, the lethality curves established in Subtask 4.1 will guide application of
ROS generating substances. For selection of Scenedesmus mutants with small antenna phenotypes, the
Recipient will use dilute (20% light attenuation) cultures at high light intensity (500-1,000 µE/m2-s) and
high partial pressure of oxygen (pO2) (250% of saturation, typical for outdoor ponds), to provide general
guidelines. Increasing the washout rates allows take-over by mutant populations that will be adapted to
the high pH/alkalinity, high ROS, and high light phenotypes. The resulting mixed populations likely with
multiple different mutations are called ‘cultivars’. Each round of selection is expected to take about one
month.
Following a first round of selection, the Recipient will put the cultivars iteratively through a second and
even a third round of mutagenesis and will select for desired phenotypes, that are faster growing
cultivars. Promising cultivars will be grown in outdoor ponds and compared to baseline strains. Only
after validation of improved performance in outdoor ponds will clonal isolates be obtained. Performance
of isolated clones can be further tested indoors in bubble columns to compare growth rates and
productivity. This is the central approach in this project to achieve the high productivity goal.
2.4.4.1. Establishing the UV Mutagenesis Procedures
The development of mutants with improved productivities began using the established UV mutagenesis
procedures.
Based on the results of the mutagenesis experiment detailed above, UV exposures resulting in a 20-90%
reduction in viability for MBE501-12 and UTEX 393 were selected for the development of pigment
mutants. Strains were randomly mutagenized following the procedure described above. Following UV
exposure, the cultures were transferred into 50 ml tubes and kept in the dark for 24 hours to prevent
photoreactivation. Samples from all time exposures were combined and plated on BG11+1.5% agar
containing antibiotics. Approximately 45 plates for each strain were plated with an estimated 1,000 cells
and initially exposed to a light intensity of 10 µmol/m2/sec. After 24-48 hours, the light intensity was
increased to between 32-40 µmol/m2/sec. Two rounds of mutagenesis were completed and plated by the
end of the reporting period.

2.4.4.2. Adaptive Laboratory Evolution with AAC Photobioreactors
For ALE strain development, cultures of both platform strains MBE501-12 and UTE X393 were grown
up, independently UV-mutagenized in multiple batches to achieve a broad range of 20-90% lethality. The
individually mutagenized batches were then mixed for recovery in the dark and following transferred into
bottles for initial growth in the light (=starter cultures). On December 11, 2023 the starter cultures were
used to inoculate one reactor (R1) with strain MBE501-12 UV-mutants and one reactor (R3) was
inoculated with UTEX 393 UV-mutants. The reactor setup is shown below (Figure 74 below).
The reactors were operated with a day/night cycle of 16 hours light at 300 μMol photons/m2-sec followed
by a dark period of 8 hours. BG11 medium with 5mEq alkalinity was used to mimic the reclaimed water
at the wastewater treatment site in Las Cruces, NM. The temperature was kept constant at 25 degree
Celsius. To achieve conditions of CO2 limitations similar to those encountered in ponds outdoors initially
only air was bubbled through the reactors. The ROS chemical Hydrogen Peroxide (H2O2) was added to
the media at a final concentration of 0.003% with every dilution of the culture.

Figure 74: Laboratory Adaptive Evolution (ALE) in ACC reactors. Shown is a side view of the AAC
photobioreactor system. The system comprises three 500 mL square glass bottles that can be separately
operated regarding temperature and light conditions. Irradiance is provided from the right side of the
bottles through LED arrays. The pH is measured for each reactor independently using probes suspended
into the medium through the lid.
Culture parameters such as pH and overall health of the algae including growth were monitored. The pH
and the optical density were recorded constantly. The system was set up for batch culture mode.
Microscopy was performed multiple times per week when cultures were diluted.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                            Page 102 of 191

Figure 75 shows changes in the pH occurred over time during cultivation. Increases in the pH in the
presence of algae indicates the uptake of CO2 from the medium during photosynthesis happening during
the light period. As the algae grow, the rate of increase in pH during the day changes.

Figure 75: Visualization of the Laboratory Adaptive Evolution (ALE) in ACC reactor using the example
of strain UTEX 393. Left – The red triangle indicates the start of the culture. The pH was plotted over
time between 12/19/2023 to 12/22/2023. The pH rose during the day when the algae removed CO2 from
the medium. The pH dropped at night as algae respired and consequently CO2 accumulated again in the
medium by diffusion from the air bubbled through the reactor. Note that the pH did not go above 11,
because the algae stopped growth as the pH in the medium reached that value. Right - Shown is an image
of the culture in the reactor.
When the cultures achieved an optical density of over 0.4, the pH increased and reached almost 11 during
the day, indicative of severe CO2 limitation. It is noteworthy that both strains of the species S. obliquus
could withstand such high pH, though at low or no productivity. Culture health was monitored by
microscopy.
ALE continued with both strains MBE501-12 and UTEX 393 throughout the entire quarter. In addition,
the UV-Mutagenesis derived pigment mutants MBE-501-12 #9, #10, and #12 were used as a mixture
(cultivar) to begin ALE (Figure 76 below), because they had been identified to outperform the platform
strain MBE501-12 in bubbling columns.
The AAC Reactor #1 contained the cultivar obtained by UV-Mutagenesis of the strain MBE501-12
(Figure 76). Reactor #3 contained the cultivar obtained by UV-Mutagenesis of the strain UTEX 393
Figure 76). Both cultivars were started in December 2023 and were under selection for over three months.
AAC Reactor #2 contained the UV-Mutagenesis derived pigment mutants MBE-501-12 #9, #10, and #12
that were used as a mixture to begin the cultivar selection (Figure 76). This cultivar was started in January
2024 and is under selection for over two months.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                              Page 103 of 191

Figure 76: Laboratory Adaptive Evolution (ALE) in ACC reactors. Shown is a side view of the AAC
photobioreactor system with three different cultures, Reactor 1 with a cultivar of MBE501-12, Reactor 2
with the pigment mutants cultivar, Reactor 3 with cultivar derived from UTEX 393.
AAC reactor cultures were diluted when they became too dense. Selection for high resistance to high pH
and reactive oxygen species continued for several months without culture restarts. An example for ALE in
ACC reactors is presented in Figure 77 for the cultivar of UTEX 393.
The pH of cultures in the reactors increases as they perform photosynthesis using the available CO2.
Consequently, increases in pH during the day indicated photosynthesis, which is related to biomass
productivity in the cultures. Reduction of the pH at night indicated respiration and CO2 ingassing.
Culture dilution was done with BG11 medium containing 5mEq alkalinity, which had a pH of about 9.0.
aliquots of the AAC reactor cultures was kept in Erlenmeyer flasks to have backup cultures in case any
AAC reactor culture might have crashed. However, the cultures were stable and did not require restarts.

strains/cultivars/pigment mutants. All cultures reached comparable densities indicating similar
productivity.
Biomass density was determined for cultures on 01/25/2024 (Figure 80 left side). As all cultures were
started with very similar biomass densities, the biomass density in the columns on 01/25/2024 can be used
to determine biomass productivity for the different strains/cultivars/pigment mutants.
It is our experience that pigment mutants created by UV mutagenesis often have reduced robustness
and/or biomass productivity when compared to the platform strain. This reduced performance of pigment
mutants is caused by many mutations that arise in cells when UV mutagenesis happens. Each cell may
have hundreds to thousands of mutations, depending on the severity of the exposure to UV. With many
mutations per cell, mutations with deleterious effects may outweigh any positive mutation that may have
caused higher performance. Consequently, screening of many mutants and selection is required to create
well performing strains.
Given the background of many mutations per cell, any low pigment mutant performing as well as the wild
type under laboratory conditions is expected to outperform the platform strain when cultivated in ponds
outdoors. The reason is that outdoor pond cultures of platform strains are strongly light limited, a
condition which provides advantages to mutants which have lighter pigmentation because many more
photons can be utilized in the pond with lighter pigmented mutant strains.
In summary, it was demonstrated that some novel pigment mutants had higher or similar biomass
productivities in Bubbling Columns in the laboratory. In particular, the MBE501-12 mutants #7, #9, #10,
and #12 stood out. Further, the cultivar of MBE501-12 is robust and performed similar to the platform
strain, an indication that it may outperform the platform strain outdoors in ponds.

2.4.4.4. Adaptive Laboratory Evolution with AAC Photobioreactors
Automated Algae Cultivation (AAC) was continued through 4/23/24 with MBE501-12 UV Treated,
MBE501-12 pigment mutants 9,10,12 and UTEX393 UV Treated cultures. Cultures underwent biweekly
dilutions and hydrogen peroxide additions to achieve a 0.003% concentration, a parameter established
previously for both MBE501-12 and UTEX 393. Cultures were maintained until the end of April when
operations ended.
Figure 81 below shows an example for the pH of the UTEX 393 culture.

2.4.5. Subtask 4.3. - Novel Strain Development Cultivar/Clonal Isolates Tests
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will continue strain improvement in the laboratory. More
iterative rounds of mutagenesis will be performed. Potentially improved cultivars and potential clones
will be re-mutagenized to stack traits and create even better cultivars/clones.
The task of strain development was continued in summer 2024 by performing UV-mutagenesis on the
platform strains as well as on one previously identified pigment mutant.
An intern at MicroBio Engineering worked on developing and identifying more pigment mutants for
MBE501-12 and UTEX393 using a similar protocol described in Subtask 4.2. (BP1; M4-15), Novel
Strain Development Using ALE Technology.
Using the previously established protocols for UV mutagenesis, the platform strains UTEX 393 and
MBE501-12 were used to generate novel pigment mutants. At the same time the novel pigment mutant
#PM09 was also used to create further mutants with improved phenotypes.
Following UV-Mutagenesis cells were spread on solidified BG11 growth medium in Petri dishes. After
about two weeks of cultivation, colonies became visible. Examples of such plates are shown in the Figure
82 below.

Figure 82: Photo of colonies growing on solidified BG11 medium. Potential pigment mutants were
identified by their change in colony coloration.
Following identification of potential pigment mutants aliquots of cells were plated for direct comparison
of the different phenotypes of the novel mutants (Figure 83 below).

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                           Page 109 of 191

Figure 83: Shown are photos of exemplary plates with potential novel pigment mutants derived from UV-
treated platform strain UTEX393 (upper row), MBE501-12 (lower row right), and pigment mutant #9
(lower row left).

Overall, 250 potential novel pigment mutants were identified visually based on the altered color of the
colonies. Following, potential pigment mutants were cultured on spot plates to assess variations in
pigmentation. Two separate screening trials were conducted to evaluate phenotypic differences and
productivity. Visual screening in the process shown through exemplary Petri dishes in Figure 83 above
resulted in a reduction of that initial number of pigment mutants to less than 25 novel strains.
In October and November 2024, multiple novel pigment mutant strains were tested for stability and
performance in bubbling columns in comparison to the parental strains. Platform strain MBE5012-12
derived mutants D3, E1, E2, F1, F2 were tested. In addition, the PM09 pigment mutant derived strain H1
was tested.
Selected mutants were initially grown in sterile BG11 medium (5 mEq/L) alongside the platform
MBE501-12 and MBE501-12 PM09 strains. The cultures were split into duplicate columns, each at an
initial optical density of 0.1 OD750, and the total volume was adjusted to 800 mL with fresh medium.
The abiotic conditions were carefully controlled, maintaining a water bath temperature of 25°C, light
intensity of 300 µmol/m²/s, and a 16:8 light:dark period. pH levels were monitored continuously using
probes placed randomly in biotic columns, with one abiotic column serving as a control. The pH in the
biotic columns was maintained between 7.5 and 8.0. Optical density (OD750) and total suspended
solids/volatile suspended solids (TSS/VSS) were measured biweekly to track culture productivity.

Figure 84: Shown are exemplary bubbling columns from testing with the novel pigment mutants D3, E1,
E2, F1, F2, and Q2.

Biomass concentrations were measured multiple times during cultivation in the bubbling columns.
Resulting biomass productivities were plotted and are presented in Figure 85.

Figure 85: Biomass productivities were plotted for a variety of pigment mutants strains. The platform
strain MBE501-12 and the 2nd generation improved strain PM09 were used to compare performance. All
pigment mutant strains had lower biomass productivity than the parental strains after startup, but then the
pigment mutants outperformed the platform strain MBE501-12 after cultures were diluted.

In summary, a variety of pigment mutants with strong phenotype were tested and many outperformed the
parental MBE501-12. Following novel pigment mutants were used for ALE strain improvement.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                            Page 111 of 191

As the mass culture pond environment presents challenges regarding reactive oxygen species due to high
oxygen concentrations in the cultivation medium, a new experiment was designed to select for resistance
to hydrogen peroxide. Several strains including novel pigment mutants were first grown up in bubbling
columns and then exposed to hydrogen peroxide at levels that were previously shown to kill cells at a rate
of >90%.
For this round of ALE, the platform strain MBE501-12 and several derived pigment mutant strains
(PMH1, PM09 and PMQ1) were cultivated in bubbling columns. Over the course of several weeks,
cultures were dosed multiple times with hydrogen peroxide to induce selection for resistance to ROS.
Doses were administered when greening of the cultures indicated recovery from previous exposures.
In general, following addition of hydrogen peroxide the cultures bleached significantly. It took multiple
days for cultures to recover and visibly show growth by renewed greening. The mutant PM09 showed
best recovery as indicated by the fastest greening following multiple doses of hydrogen peroxide. This
may have been be due to a genetic change resulting in a novel trait that confers cells resistance to ROS.
As multiple possible mutations could produce resistance in this selection process, resulting resistant
cultures will be designated as cultivars. These cultivars will be scaled up into ponds to test if the novel
cultivars will be able to also perform in the outdoor environment.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                               Page 112 of 191

2.5. Task 5.0 - S. obliquus Genomics for CUE and Productivity
2.5.1. Background for Task 5.0
Establishing a high-resolution reference genome via whole-genome sequencing provides the essential
genetic baseline required to systematically enhance CUE and biomass productivity. By annotating the
chromosomal architecture, this map converts the platform strain of S. obliquus into an actionable
metabolic blueprint, pinpointing specific gene targets and regulatory networks that govern
photosynthetic efficiency, inorganic carbon fixation, and cellular growth dynamics.
The reference map will serve as the foundation for rapid, non-GMO strain development utilizing random
UV mutagenesis. UV exposure creates random mutations within the genome that will impact the
phenotype of improved strains. Resequencing UV-irradiated candidates against the baseline genome
enables precise tracking and profiling of random genetic variations, distinguishing high-impact driver
mutations in carbon-capture and assimilation pathways from neutral background mutations. This
genome-guided approach will drastically accelerate the strain development pipeline, replacing lengthy
phenotypic trial-and-error with rapid genotypic validation to swiftly isolate and deploy fast-growing,
highly productive microalgal strains optimized for carbon utilization.
2.5.2. Objectives for Task 5.0
The main objective for this task was identification of mutations that created specific phenotypes.
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will characterize genomes of the baseline strain and select
mutants to determine and validate the genetic changes underpinning the improved traits. Sequencing of
two populations will be completed following mutagenesis to compare baseline mutations between the
platform strains.
2.5.3. Subtask 5.1. - Genome Sequencing of Both Platform Strains
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will construct a reference grade genome assembly for the
baseline S. obliquus strains MBE 501-12 and UTEX 393. High molecular weight DNA will be extracted
using established methods. The resulting DNA will be sequenced using PacBio technology to maximize
the accuracy and to create chromosome level scaffolds. If needed, High-Throughput Chromosome
Conformation Capture (Hi-C) short read data/proximity maps will be generated to separate and order
the haplotypes. The resulting genome assembly will be annotated using a customized pipeline based on
previous annotations. The Recipient has the gap-less genome of strain UTEX3031 at hand, but strain 501-
12 originated from the ALE process. A novel and improved genome of strain UTEX393 is available for the
Recipient. However, re-sequencing may be needed as the genome sequence was constructed several
years ago.
S. obliquus strains MBE 501-12 and UTEX 393 were revived from stock cultures. Cultures were started
for generation of biomass for sequencing.
Initially, biomass was produced for three strains: the UV-mutagenesis derived platform strain MBE501-
12 and wild type platform strain UTEX 393. Biomass from all three strains was forwarded to LANL for
reference sequencing of the genome.
Genomic DNA was extracted using a standard DNA extraction methods developed at LANL (agar-based
enzymatic protoplasting followed by gentle lysis). DNA extracts were tested for quality and concentration
on Tapestation 5.0 and Qubit 5.0. DNA was slightly fragmented but of sufficient quality for DNA
sequencing. Library preparation was performed using the NEBNext DNA Library Prep Kit for Illumina

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                           Page 113 of 191

(New England Biolabs) according to standard protocols. In brief, sequenced samples were processed by
DNA fragmentation, end repair, adaptor ligation, and size selection prior to NEBNext amplification and
PCR AMPure cleanup. Libraries were tested using Tapestation 5.0 and Qubit 5.0 prior to library
normalization and pooling and sequencing on the Illumina NextSeq 500 (paired end 150BP sequencing).
The raw sequencing data obtained from the NextSeq 500 platform were subjected to quality control using
fastp to assess the overall quality of the reads and remove adapter sequences and low-quality bases (Q-
score < 20). Cleaned reads were aligned to the reference genome using BWA-MEM (version 0.7.17).
The resulting alignment files (BAM files) were then used as input for DeepVariant (version 1.5.0).
DeepVariant generated variant calling format files (VCF files) were used as input for custom analysis in
R using “GenomicFeatures” (1.50.3), “GenomicRanges” (1.50.2) and “VariantAnnotation” (1.44.0)
packages.
Variants were filtered to identify a subset of detected variations that are community-dominant (Variant
Allele Frequence = 1). The filtered VCF file was indexed using GATK IndexFeatureFile function. The
reference (DOE 152Z) genome was indexed (samtools) to process to produce a dictionary file (.dict)
using the PicardTools CreateSequenceDictionary. The reference genome, dictionary, filtered VCF, and
their respective indices were used to produce at chromosome-level, gapless reference genome for the
MBE501-12 incorporating the community-dominant variants.

As the genome for the diploid wild type strain UTEX 3031 had just been completed at a chromosomal
level when this project began (Biondi et al 2024, 10.1016/j.algal.2024.103431) it was used as the gold
standard and work focused on the platform strain MBE501-12 as it was previously derived from UTEX
3031 by UV-mutagenesis.
The genome of the original strain UTEX 3031 (=DOE0152Z) is gapless, but it is a diploid genome, more
information about the genome of derived strain MBE501-12 is presented below. The strain MBE501-12
strain had been created by UV-Mutagenesis previously during the DOE funded ABY2 project. It had been
isolated after numerous rounds of selection for fast growth.
As UV-Mutagenesis has a preference for biallelic base changes, the frequency of those base changes was
analyzed first and is presented in Figure 86 below. Numerous variants were detected in the community of
MBE501-12 as was anticipated based on UV mutagenesis.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                          Page 114 of 191

Figure 86: Each of the different panels represent changes from the reference base to the other three bases
and their contributions to the variants detected.

Figure 87: The genome of MBE501-12 is diploid. The Variant Allele Frequencies were determined and
are shown. Left - overall count. Center - Heterozygote. Right - Homozygote.
A total of 63 variants met strict inclusion criteria (i.e. located in promoters or coding regions) having a
variant allele frequency (VAF) of 1 (community-dominating variant) and a high quality of call (Q > 30,
99.9% confidence in call). These included 27 synonymous, 33 non-synonymous, 2 nonsense, and 1
frameshift mutations (Table 20). While 20 of these occurred in unknown genes (14 non-synonymous, 1
frameshift, 1 nonsense, 4 synonymous) many targeted genes are known. Of special interest are the 8
mutations targeting CD4A (an ATP-dependent Clp protease ATP-binding subunit, chloroplastic,
Sobl_g09268) with two non-synonymous.

2.6.1. Background to Biostimulants Testing
Biostimulants from seaweeds, humic acids, protein hydrolysates, bacteria, and microalgal products
represent a rapidly growing area in regenerative agricultural applications.
2.6.2. Objectives for Task 6.0
Determining the ability of the S. obliquus-derived product to enhance crop performance is the primary
objective of this task.
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will use wet biomass (15%-20% solids) produced in Task 2 in
greenhouse biostimulant performance trials and soil microbiome activation trials for effects on model
crops.
2.6.3. Subtask 6.1. - Biostimulant Product Testing in Greenhouse with Crops
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will create multiple whole-cell biomass samples to be tested
in greenhouse trials and soil microbiome activation trials as soil amendment co-products.
Analytical testing of biomass for quality includes the initial screening spectrum of the following:
compositional analysis, bioactives analysis, amino acid profile, lipid profile, pathogen analysis, heavy
metal analysis, and any other analytical testing as required based upon what is determined in the initial
screening.
Full evaluation of a product requires product effectiveness evaluation in both a Greenhouse Testing
(GHT) platform and Soil Microbiome Activation Testing (SMAT) platform. The GHT platform evaluates
products applied via soil drench, foliar, and/or seed treatment. The potential product(s) will be compared
to standard controls in terms of root development and size, shoot development and size, germination
rate, time to emergence, and other key plant health indicators such as improved nitrogen utilization.
Biomass was produced in 1,000L ponds at the field site in San Luis Obispo, CA. The algal biomass was
immediately frozen and sent to Heliae, where the experiments under this Task were carried out .
 1. S. obliquus (MBE501-12), 1.7kg consisting of 21% solids.
 2. S. obliquus (UTEX393), 1.1 kg consisting of 24% solids.
Received biomass from subtask 2.1 of two algal strains, S. obliquus UTEX 393 and MBE501-12. Each
biomass sample was formulated into products for drench application and seed treatment testing in the
GHT and SMAT platforms.
The soil drench product consisted of whole cell biomass at a concentration of 10% solids, while the seed
treatment product consisted of mechanically lysed biomass at 10% solids. Run 1 of soil drench testing
was initiated using tomato, cauliflower and lettuce crops with 1%, 2.5% and 5% application rates. Run 1
of the seed treatment testing was initiated with corn with application rates of 2 oz/100 lb seeds and 5
oz/100 lb seeds.

2.7. Task 7.0 – TEA/LCA Studies and Model Development
2.7.1 Background
Techno-economic analysis (TEA) and life cycle assessment (LCA) studies evaluating S. obliquus as a
feedstock for agricultural biostimulant will provide an outlook on commercial feasibility and the
bottlenecks regarding economic viability. Expanding system boundaries to a cradle-to-farm-gate scale
may demonstrates that biological extracts from S. obliquus yield net-environmental benefits by improving
field nutrient uptake and offsetting synthetic fertilizer consumption.
2.7.2 Objectives for Task 7.0
The objective of the TEA and LCA was to evaluate the commercial feasibility, economic drivers, and
environmental sustainability of utilizing S. obliquus as an agricultural biostimulant feedstock across
scaled production facilities (40 ha and 400 ha). By modeling process economics as a function of CUE
with CO2 sourcing, and extending system boundaries to a cradle-to-farm-gate framework. The task aims
to identify cost bottlenecks and quantify net-environmental benefits derived from enhanced field nutrient
uptake and synthetic fertilizer displacement.
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will develop TEA and LCA models, which will then be refined
throughout the project for both an initial (e.g., 40 ha, 100 acre) and a large-scale nth plant algae
production facility of 400 ha (1,000 acres) with 10 acre (4-ha) open raceways. These will be compared
with similar studies by DOE Bioenergy Technologies Office (BETO) (including for the 100 acre Sapphire
facility in NM built almost 15 years ago, for which detailed cost estimates are available, both for
alternative designs and actual plant construction costs). The TEA models will then be updated with data
acquired during this project to output economics of the photoautotrophic process, as a function of CUE
and CO2 cost (e.g., flue gas vs. merchant). The TEA and LCA will be performed in accordance with SOPO
Appendices C and D.
2.7.3. Subtask 7.1. - Incorporate Initial Experimental Data into TEA/LCA and Process Models
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will input data into prior, but updated, TEA and LCA models
to develop current process economic models for the standard algae farm (400 ha, merchant CO2) and
update existing TEA and LCA models in EXCEL and openLCA, for the envisioned bioproducts
(biostimulants, other bioenhancement products for agriculture) facility at scale. The Recipient will import
the latest geographically and temporally Life Cycle Inventory (LCI) datasets into the openLCA model,
evaluate and update existing mechanisms for sensitivity analyses, scope the proposed process to
appropriately indicate intended boundaries for the integrated TEA and LCA.
The other major aspect of this subtask will be to develop process models for such large-scale cultivation
systems and how these will integrate the CUE and CO2 Utilization Efficiency (CO2UE) outcomes from
these studies. Current commercial and larger-scale production systems (40 to 400 ha) will be addressed
through process modeling, as experiments with small ponds do not scale directly to large ponds. The
objective of this project is not to achieve a high CUE in small ponds, but to develop data and models that
would allow application to large-scale production systems.
The TEA/LCA work was begun with a kick-off meeting that was held on November 29th 2023. The
initial TEA based on prior work by the MBE team, was reviewed for updates required to adapt to the
present process and projected outcomes. The initial TEA/LCA work was based on the projected project

outcomes, in terms of productivity and CUEs, and applied to the conditions in NM, to include two scales
and CUEs for 40 and 400 ha and CUEs of 40 and 80%. See Table 26 below.
These TEA studies were based on a consumptive use of discharged wastewaters, appropriate for semi-arid

In summary, the preliminary TEA and LCA demonstrated that process optimization could dramatically
enhance both the financial viability and environmental sustainability of microalgal biostimulant
production. Transitioning from baseline to improved processing scenarios could reduce plant-gate
production costs by 38–43% (reaching $980/tonne at a 400-ha scale) while simultaneously cutting Global
Warming Potential (GWP) emissions by 62% (from 6.1 to 2.3 kg CO₂e/kg biostimulant). While
production costs benefit significantly from economies of scale (40 ha vs. 400 ha), decarbonization is
predominantly driven by process improvements, specifically enhancing CUE to suppress CO2 off-gassing.
2.7.4. Subtask 7.2. - Final TEA/LCS studies
Task Description from SOPO: The Recipient will continue TEA/LCA studies based on the models created
in BP1, updated with new data.
MBE built a TEA for a n-th level 1,000-acre algae production facility co-located (~8km) from a natural
gas combined cycle powerplant (NGCC). Harvesting technology selected for this facility were gravity
settlers followed by centrifugation to reach total solids of ~8-10%. Due to the projected end-use of bio-
stimulants/soil amendments, the ponds were specified to be partially lined which reduced construction
costs significantly. Following BETO nth plant assumptions and an operating period of 20 years, the
minimum selling price (MSP) of the biomass produced was determined in a DCFROR analysis to be
$778. This model accounts for a biomass productivity of 15 g/m2/day, and a CUE of 65%, but only serves
as a baseline for future expansion as data from CRIAS pilot research provides more specific input
variables.

See Appendix B for the final TEA report and see Appendix C for the final LCA report.

3. CONCLUSION AND RESEARCH NEEDS
The Cultivation-Ready Improved Algae Strains (CRIAS) project successfully delivered novel biological
and engineering solutions, fundamentally addressing key challenges in CUE and biomass productivity in
algal cultivation. The project met or exceeded its core objectives, validating a viable commercial pathway
for producing low-carbon, cost-effective algal biomass for agricultural bioproducts.
A major advantage of this integrated technology over conventional microalgae production is that the
harvested biomass does not require thermal drying, significantly lowering operating costs and overcoming
a primary historical constraint in the commercialization of unicellular and small colonial microalgae.
3.1. Microalgae for Bioenhancers
The specific commercial application advanced by this project is the use of the algal biomass as a premium
bioenhancer for regenerative agriculture, either as a biostimulant for market entry and possibly as
biofertilizer once large production volumes will be reached.
Regarding product efficacy in regenerative agriculture, testing confirmed the efficacy of S. obliquus
biomass as a bioenhancer. Drench testing showed positive performance regarding shoot and root biomass
in plant species tested in the greenhouse. Furthermore, an enhancement on harvest yield in bushels/acre
was demonstrated for winter wheat in field trials, although this positive effect appeared location
dependent. Soil Microbiome Activation testing also yielded positive results.
3.2. Strain Development of Microalgae
Algae Strain Development and Productivity: Stable cultivation of the several novel strains was
demonstrated for over 30 days at field sites and in 1,000 Liter testbed ponds in California and New
Mexico. Novel strains, created through non-GMO UV-Mutagenesis and ALE, exhibited improved
productivity, including one strain (ALE01) that showed about 30% higher biomass productivity than the
platform strain during part of the 30-day experiment. Four iterative rounds of UV-Mutagenesis allowed
for trait stacking without a loss in biomass productivity.
Genomics and Strain Characterization: Research successfully determined that UV mutagenesis in S.
obliquus induces short indels, and the underlying mutations causing improved phenotypes are possible to
determine using a high-quality genome.
3.3. Carbon Utilization Efficiency in Cultivation of Microalgae
The target CUE of 70% was successfully surpassed. Novel saturators for improved CO2 delivery
significantly increased the CUE to more than 80% as compared to the traditional pond sparging reaching
only about 40-50% CUE.
3.4 Process Scale up and Economics of Microalgae Production
The economic viability or our overall process was confirmed by a TEA, which indicated algal feedstock
biomass costs of <$1,000 per ton for an intermediate scenario, with a best-case potential of <$600 per ton
biomass. The LCA demonstrated a 5 to 10 fold improvement over the current industry standard, affirming
the technology's strong environmental performance and potential for a circular economy approach.
3.5. Summary of Achievements of Project Success Criteria
In summary, the integration of biological (improved S. obliquus strains) and engineering (novel gas-to-
liquid transfer) innovations has resulted in a synergistic system that successfully validated the technical
potential for low-carbon, high-efficiency, and cost-competitive algal biomass production.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                              Page 134 of 191

3.6. R&D Gaps and Research Needs
Basic demonstration of our innovations in biology and engineering paved the pathway to
commercialization. However, our processes were only demonstrated at small scale. The need for large-
scale production plants (>100 hectares) and ponds (>1 ha each) for economic viability dictates
consideration of the scalability of core processes with several crucial areas still requiring advances for
cost-effective implementation:
1. CO2 Injection at Scale: CO2 injection stations are a major capital and operating cost, and there are
 currently no commercial facilities operating at significant scale using flue gas CO2 sources.
 Alternative CO2 injection systems also have limitations at large scales.
2. Pond management: Stability of cultures for >30 days was proven, but pest pressure exists and was
 detrimental to prolonged cultivation, in particular during summer time.
3. Phenotype stability of novel strains: We demonstrated for the first time that strains with novel
 phenotypes could be scaled into 1,000 Liter ponds outdoors. However, it was noticed that several
 strains lost their phenotypes over time in the ponds. This may be a characteristic of the overall
 approach, because novel mutants were selected for in the laboratory. Future research may have to
 focus on Adaptive Pond Evolution with real-world outdoor selection in ponds rather than in
 bioreactors mimicking outdoor conditions.
4. Genomics: Further genomic studies to characterize the genetic basis for the enhanced phenotypes,
 which will be essential for future strain development.

3.7. Concluding Remarks
The conclusion from this project is that the production of the green alga S. obliquus for bioenhancers
using low-cost waste sources of CO2 from point sources such as wastewater treatment plants is a highly
promising technology when our innovations in biology and carbon utilization will be integrated.

 Final Technical Report – September 8, 2026 - MicroBio Engineering Inc.
                                                                                              Page 135 of 191

1. INTRODUCTION
This Techno-Economic Analysis (TEA) report provides a comprehensive evaluation of the “Cultivation-
Ready Improved Algae Strains (CRIAS) for Increased Carbon Utilization Efficiency” technology
platform, modeled at both a commercial scale of 400 hectares (approximately 1,000 acres) and at a
demonstration scale of 40 ha. The analysis uses data derived from laboratory research, pilot-scale field
trials, and detailed process engineering models developed by the MicroBio Engineering Inc. (MBE) team,
to project the economic viability of producing Scenedesmus obliquus for use as agricultural biostimulants.
The primary technical objective of the CRIAS project is to demonstrate a pathway to exceed the DOE’s
Carbon Utilization Efficiency (CUE) target of 50%, aiming for a system-wide CUE of 70% or greater
while achieving high productivity. This is achieved through a synergistic approach that combines pH-
tolerant algal strains with a specialized gas-to-liquid transfer system designed to utilize dilute flue gas
from biogas combustion sources efficiently.
The facilities are modelled as an “Nth of a Kind” (NOAK) commercial facility to identify the
fundamental economic and process drivers requires to achieve market competitiveness at a mature
commercial scale. The analysis integrates financial modeling with detailed process simulation, providing
a granular view of capital expenditures (CAPEX), operating expenditures (OPEX), and the resulting
Minimum Biomass Selling Price (MBSP). Furthermore, the CRIAS process is compared to an algae
cultivation facility operating within standard industry conditions. An accompanying life cycle assessment
(LCA) report details the environmental impacts of the improved system.
1.1 Scope of Analysis
This report evaluates the techno-economic performance of a hypothetical algal production facility located
in the American Southwest, specifically modeled based on meteorological data, land use costs, and
industrial electricity cost from New Mexico, a region characterized by high solar insolation and relatively
favorable temperatures for year-round cultivation. The facility is to be co-located with a utility partner: a
biogas combustion power plant, which supplies the necessary CO2 in the form of flue gas as well as
process water for media make-up. An ideal scenario for this model would also include a municipal
wastewater treatment plant (WWTP) providing reclaimed water for cultivation as it would reduce both
nutrient and freshwater burden. However, such locations and scales for the present analysis are rare and
the present LCA and TEA assume freshwater use (from the same source as the powerplant cooling water)
and supply of agricultural fertilizers for algae nutrients.
The output of this system is dewatered biomass at 10% w/w to be used as a biostimulant. The processing,
storage and distribution of the biomass for such applications is not included in this TEA (Figure 1). To
note, current commercial biostimulants are typically sold at this concentration, are minimally processed
(pH is typically reduced to inhibit anaerobic fermentation, to allow storage for up to two years), prior to
shipping to farmers for applications [1].

Within the TEA boundaries are:
 • Raw Material Acquisition: The sourcing and transport of flue gas, water, and nutrients
 (agricultural fertilizers).
 • Cultivation: The construction and operation of 400 hectares of High-Rate Algal Ponds (HRAPs)
 with specialized flue gas contact sumps designed for maximum photosynthetic efficiency and
 carbon retention.
 • Inoculum Production: The maintenance and scale-up of pure algal seed cultures to support the
 large-scale production ponds.
 • Harvesting and Dewatering: The separation of algal biomass from the bulk culture medium to
 achieve a final solids concentration of 10% ash-free dry weight (AFDW).

 • Ancillary Systems: All supporting infrastructure, including electrical distribution, water
 circulation networks, and on-site storage.

  Figure 1 - Block flow diagram of the proposed CRIAS process, with the TEA boundary marked in
 dashed lines

Setting the boundary at the facility gate allows this analysis to remain flexible regarding end-product
formulations.
This system is compared to the industry-standard of algae cultivation, which is a nearly identical facility
in terms of construction. The only difference in installed cost is that the industry-standard includes liquid
CO2 storage onsite for merchant CO2 use instead of flue gas distribution as in this case [2]. However,
operating conditions are different, as detailed in later sections.
1.2 Technical Basis and Innovation Areas
The techno-economic model is grounded in three core innovation areas developed under the CRIAS
project, which fundamentally alters the cost structure compared to conventional algal production
baselines.
Innovation Area I: High CUE Process via Alkalinity Management
Standard algal cultivation systems typically operate at neutral to slightly alkaline pH (7.0–8.5 and rely on
the continuous injection of pure CO2 to maintain pH and carbon sufficiency. This approach is often
inefficient, resulting in significant CO2 outgassing losses and high operational costs [3]. The CRIAS
process innovates by operating at elevated pH levels (up to 9.0) and high alkalinity. Under these
conditions, the inorganic carbon equilibrium shifts toward bicarbonate (HCO3) and carbonate (CO3).
This "chemical trapping" mechanism significantly increases the retention of sparged CO2 in the liquid
phase, thereby enhancing CUE and reducing the parasitic energy and cost associated with CO2 supply.
Innovation Area II: Advanced Strain Development
To exploit the high-pH operational strategy, the project utilizes improved strains of Scenedesmus obliquus
(specifically strains UTEX 393 and the evolved MBE 501-12). These strains have been developed
through Adaptive Laboratory Evolution (ALE) and genomic analysis to thrive in high-alkalinity
environments that would be inhibitory to many wild-type algae. Key phenotypic traits selected for include
tolerance to high dissolved oxygen concentrations (reducing the need for energy-intensive degassing),
truncated light-harvesting antennae (improving light penetration and photosynthetic efficiency in dense
cultures), and robustness against variable environmental conditions. The baseline productivity assumption
for the TEA is an annual average of 15 grams of ash-free dry weight per square meter per day (15 g
AFDW/m2/day), with sensitivity/uncertainty scenarios exploring the impact of maintaining 20 g/m2/day
(along with other parameter shifts).

Innovation Area III: Integration with Waste Streams
The economic viability of the facility is heavily dependent on minimizing variable operating costs. By
utilizing flue gas from biogas combustion (approx. 10-12% CO2, higher than the 3-4% associated with
natural gas power plants [4]) instead of purified merchant CO2, the facility avoids the high energy and
capital costs associated with carbon capture and liquefaction. Waste stream integration with a reclaimed
water source would further lower costs but would limit possible sites and therefore is not modelled.

2. PROCESS DESIGN AND ENGINEERING DESCRIPTION
2.1 40-ha Demonstration Scale Facility and Inoculum Production Plant
The demonstration facility described herein would be constructed and operated prior to the full-scale 400
ha facility to derisk such a first-of-its-kind-plant. The commercial viability of this demonstration scale
plant would depend on the specific biostimulants markets addressed.
See Figure 2 for the 40-ha demonstration plant layout. This plant would, after the demonstration phase
has been completed, serve as the inoculum production plant for the full-scale 400 ha facility (“Area 200”
in this economic analysis). The 2-hectare (5-acre) raceway ponds are arranged to minimize piping
distances for water, CO2 and power distribution, for accessibility for maintenance, vehicles etc. Costs for
the demonstration-scale facility are presented in Section 2.4 below.

Figure 2 - Proposed layout of the 40-ha demonstration pond facility. The power plant provides flue gas
for CO2 as well as power for operations. The minor blowdown from the algae farm can be supplied back
 to the utility or recycled. The make-up for evaporation and blow-down water requirements are provided
 from local surface water or reclaimed wastewater. The ponds are arranged to reduce costs through
 shortest distributions of flue gas, water and power.

2.2 400-ha Site Layout
The conceptual design for the 400-hectare CRIAS facility is organized into modular blocks for
operational management, with a similar layout to the 40-ha demonstration/inoculum site in Figure 2, but

with 4-ha raceway ponds (see Figure 3, below), and excluding the smaller inoculum ponds. It includes
five such modules, each 80 ha in scale. As this technology is still at a Technology Readiness Level (TRL)
4, this design assumes no site constraints to the layout. The total facility footprint, including non-wetted
areas for berms, roads, and infrastructure, and inoculum system is estimated at about 600 hectares.
The process flow begins with the intake of reclaimed water and flue gas, which are distributed to the
cultivation ponds (Area 100) via a centralized piping network (Areas 300 and 400). The algal culture is
maintained in semi-continuous operation, with a portion of the culture volume harvested daily to maintain
optimal cell density. The harvested slurry is conveyed to distributed primary settling stations (Area 500)
located near the ponds, where settling tanks concentrate the biomass. The thickened slurry is then pumped
to a central dewatering facility for final concentration via centrifugation. The clarified supernatant from
both harvesting stages is recycled back to the cultivation ponds to conserve water and nutrients, creating a
nearly closed-loop hydraulic system with about a 10% blowdown.
2.3 Area 100: Raceway Microalgae Production Ponds
The cultivation system represents the single largest capital cost center and operational driver of the
facility. Area 100 is comprised of 100 identical raceway ponds, each with a wetted surface area of 40,000
m2 (4 hectares). The ponds utilize a "single-loop" design with a central dividing wall, minimizing the
hydraulic head loss compared to serpentine designs (Figure 3).

 Figure 3 - Standard 4-ha raceway design

Civil Engineering and Construction
Construction of the ponds involves earthwork, estimated at 1.2 million cubic meters excavation and 4
million square meters of fine (laser level) grading to achieve the precise slope and flatness required for
uniform hydraulic flow. The ponds are only partially lined, with HDPE (High-Density Polyethylene)
liners are installed only in areas subject to high fluid turbulence and potential erosion, specifically the
paddlewheel stations and CO2 transfer sumps, and at the ends of the ponds, around corners, scour zones,
and berms. The long straight sections of the raceways rely on native compacted and clay soils—along
with microalgal self-sealing—for minimal percolation [5]. This approach is required to greatly reduce
liner costs while mitigating the risks of erosion and leakage.
Mixing and Hydraulics
Mixing is provided by paddlewheels powered by 15-hp inverter-duty electric gearmotors controlled by
variable frequency drives (VFDs). The mixing system is designed to maintain a mean channel velocity of
20 to 30 cm/s, to keep algal cells in suspension, ensure uniform nutrient distribution, and minimize
stratification.

 Table 1 - Algae raceway key sizing and operational parameters.
 Parameter Value Units
 Average Hydraulic Residence Time 5 Days

 Nighttime Channel Velocity 0.2 m/s
 Daytime/Nighttime Operations 12/12 Hrs

The energy consumption for mixing is a key operational parameter; the model projects a paddlewheel
efficiency of 45%. The design includes concrete foundations for paddlewheel assemblies and sumps to
ensure structural longevity.
Carbonation System
CO2 is supplied to each pond via a specialized gas sparging system located in a deepened (1.5 m+) sump
area. The sump allows for installation of a baffle for countercurrent water-gas flow, to extend gas-liquid
contact time. (See further in Section 2.5)

 Figure 4 - Render of a carbonation sump with coarse bubble diffusers

The system utilizes bubble diffusers and operation at relatively high-pH/alkalinity with gas flow
controlled with real-time pH monitoring to optimize for carbon uptake and supply for algal growth.
2.4 Area 200: Inoculum System
The stability and productivity of the 400-hectare production facility depend entirely on a consistent
supply of high-quality algal inoculum. Area 200, after its initial function as demonstration plant, will
provide sufficient biomass of the genetically improved S. obliquus strains to allow for start-up and restart
of production ponds, with routine maintenance or unexpected culture crashes.

The inoculum system follows a tiered scale-up architecture, ensuring that the culture remains ‘pure’
(unialgal and pest and other contaminants-free) during scale-up from laboratory stock cultures to the 2-ha
inoculum (starter cultures) production ponds. The process train begins with laboratory-scale
photobioreactors (PBRs), progressing to small, covered outdoor ponds, and finally to larger, 2-ha open
ponds that serve as the direct seed source for Area 100. Unlike the production ponds, the inoculum ponds
are fully lined with HDPE to facilitate cleaning between cultivation runs and reduce risks of biological
contamination or invasive species establishment.
The Area 200 required size for the inoculum system, and thus capital and operating costs for a Nth-of-its-
kind plant (NOAK), would be much lower than the present demonstration plant, as it would not need to
be derisked to the extent of the present first-of-its-kind plant. As the present objective is to estimate the
costs of a NOAK plan, we project herein that the inoculum system costs will be at 10% of Area 100 costs,
in line with prior engineering studies by MBE. The inoculum system would allow restart of the
production ponds on average every six weeks.
2.5 Area 300: CO2 and Electrical Distribution
The efficient delivery of carbon dioxide is a cornerstone of the CRIAS techno-economic model. Area 300
encompasses the transport of flue gas from the co-located biogas power plant to the algae facility and
distribution and transfer of it into the individual ponds.
Flue Gas Transport
The flue gas is extracted downstream of the flue gas desulfurization (FGD) system to ensure the removal
of sulfur oxides before delivery to the algae cultivation ponds. (Note: absence of a FGD system would
require making up for alkalinity losses but is not further addressed herein). With a CO2 content in biogas-
derived flue gas typically over 12% by volume, the flue gas can be transported to the algae biomass
facility, herein modeled as a distance of 5 km from the power source.
High-flow, low-pressure centrifugal compressors at the powerplant maintain a delivered (downstream)
pressure of 20 psig to the entry of the 400-hectare facility. Variable frequency drives (VFDs) are utilized
to modulate compressor speed, optimizing energy consumption during periods of low carbon demand.
The process includes liquid knockouts installed upstream for initial condensation collection. The
compressed gas is then cooled to 35°F (2°C) in a refrigerant-type cycling air dryer to significantly lower
the dew point before being reheated to an ambient temperature of approximately 68°F (20°C) for
transport through the High-Density Polyethylene (HDPE) distribution piping.
Average and peak volumetric flow (𝑞!" ) at normal temperature and absolute pressure (NTP, 𝑇# , 𝑃#" )
conditions to size the compressors was calculated using a modified simple equation for isothermal
compressible flow (Equation 1, metric). Potential energy from gravity (𝜑) was considered negligible,
downstream pressure (𝑃$" ) and upstream pressure (𝑃%" ) were converted to absolute. The Swamee-Jain
equation (

Equation 2) for HDPE pipe was used to determine the darcy friction factor (𝑓), paired with the calculation
for Reynold’s number for laminar flow
Equation 3). Length (𝐿& ) and diameter (𝑑) of the pipe were 5km and ~ 0.7m, respectively. An assumed
increase in delivered temperature due to friction allowed for an average temperature (𝑇'() ) of 25 °C. The
amount of nitrogen in the flue gas allowed for a safe 1:1 assumption for both compressibility factor
(𝑍*,'() ) and specific gravity (𝑆) ) compared to air.

The flue gas is distributed throughout the raceway systems via an HDPE network, with delivery
controlled by actuated valves linked to pH sensors to maintain optimal aqueous chemistry. Carbonation
occurs in counter-current sumps located downstream of the paddlewheels using wastewater-treatment-
type diffusers, which incur a pressure drop of 1.5 psig. The 20-psig source pressure is sufficient to
overcome the combined resistance of the distribution system, the diffusers, and the hydraulic head of the
pond. Engineering optimization for the system involves a balance between compressor operating costs
and the capital expenditures of the pipeline and drying units. As the transport distance increases, the
capital cost of the pipeline exerts greater influence on the total annualized cost due to the larger diameters
required to manage pressure losses. By increasing the operating pressure, the system can utilize smaller-
diameter piping for flue gas transfer, a trade-off that was modeled to establish the optimal operating
pressure for achieving the targeted 75% Carbon Utilization Efficiency (CUE).
Distribution Network
Within the facility boundaries, a branched piping network distributes the flue gas to 100 individual ponds.
The capital cost for this network is substantial, driven by the sheer length of piping required to service a
400-hectare footprint. The design includes condensation traps and cleanouts to manage moisture
accumulation in the flue gas lines. Furthermore, temporary compressed flue gas storage is required for the
pond modules to equalize flue gas pressure and demand during peak
Electrical Infrastructure
Area 300 also includes the main electrical substation and site-wide distribution grid. This system powers
paddlewheel stations, harvesting pumps, media pumps, and control systems. The design accounts for the
peak power loads during daylight hours when photosynthetic activity and mixing requirements are
highest. As the ranges of loads can vary significantly, the uncertainty analysis allows for a range of
installation costs.
2.6 Area 400: Make-up Water
Water resource management in Area 400 focuses on the storage and distribution of water, for both recycle
and media top-off. As the source is assumed to be from the powerplant, the same distance for the makeup
water is assumed (5 km, L). This water provides the makeup volume required to offset evaporation, a
major factor in the arid Southwestern climate utilized for the model (annual average rainfall of roughly
0.6 mm/day vs high pan evaporation rates of 6.3 mm/day) [6]. In addition, a fraction of the harvest water

(10% of the water recycle stream) is sent to “blowdown”, e.g. final disposal, to maintain acceptable
salinity levels for cultivation of the S. obliquus strain. during the water recycling process.
The makeup water system includes intake pumps, a supply pipeline, recycle booster pumps, an on-site
buffer reservoir to manage fluctuations in supply or demand. From the reservoir, water is distributed to
the ponds via a gravity-fed canal network.
Harvesting will not be continuous, and peak flow to the ponds for a fast refill time was designed for, at
~9,750 m3/h to the site. Only major head loss (ℎ;,&'<=> ) sources were considered for the model,
calculated using Darcy-Weisbach (Equation 5). Swaimee-Jain was again used for the friction factor (𝑓),
but using a Reynolds number calculated for turbulent flow. An internal diameter (D) of ~1.2 m (4’) was
selected to give a velocity (v) of 2.5 m/s. Total head (ℎ ? ), the sum of major head losses and the elevation
change of 5 m was used to calculate the pump power (𝑃! , Equation 6).

A bowl and motor efficiency of 65% and 90%, respectively, was applied to determine the pump energy
requirement.
2.7 Area 500: Harvesting and Dewatering
Area 500 is responsible for separating the dilute algal biomass (approx. 0.5 g/L density) from the large
volume of pond water and concentrating it into a thin paste (10% ash-free dry weight solids or 100 g/L)
suitable for downstream commercial use (e/g/ as biostimulants). A two-stage, well-established dewatering
strategy designed to minimize complications is used as described next.
Primary Harvesting: Gravity Settling
The first stage utilizes 20 in-ground gravity settlers, with one settler servicing a cluster of five production
ponds. These large, earthwork structures are lined with HDPE and equipped with "Houle Alley Cat" type
sludge scrapers. The algal culture is pumped from the ponds into the settlers, where the biomass naturally
sediments out of suspension—a process enhanced by the bio-flocculating characteristics of the selected
Scenedesmus sp. strains. Gravity settling is extremely energy-efficient compared to mechanical
separation, removing 90-95% of the water volume with minimal power input [7].
Supernatant is returned to the cultivation ponds via buffer reservoirs. Cloth filters could be used if needed
at the return line to remove unsettled biomass before recycling but are not included.
Secondary Dewatering: Centrifugation
The thick subnatant from the settlers (approx. 1-2% solids) is pumped to a central processing building
housing industrial decanter centrifuges, a standard dewatering technology used in commercial algae
cultivation [2][7]. These high-speed machines provide the final dewatering step, raising the solids content
to 10%. While centrifugation is energy-intensive, its application is feasible because the volume of fluid to
be processed has reduced by approximately 30-fold.
2.8 Mass and Energy Balancing
Table 2 lists the major input parameters ranges for both the TEA and LCA, which were used to
conduct the mass balance, determine CAPEX and OPEX, and calculate life cycle impacts. Other
significant variables used for the mass balance are included in Appendix A but were left constant

across each scenario. The expected case (highlighted bold) is the case used in all following work
unless otherwise noted.

An iterative mass balance of major nutrients, solids, and flow was performed using a goal-seek macro
checked against recycling and output errors (Figure 5). As the top-off water source (influent) was
assumed to be fresh water from the powerplant, no heterotrophic growth from BOD was incorporated,
with all biomass produced photoautotrophically. The urea and diammonium phosphate (DAP) are mixed
into the media storage and add no extra water to the flow balance and are modeled in surplus (0.1%)
conditions. Carbon is supplied as scrubbed flue gas, about 10% CO2 (27% carbon content) and 90% N2,
with other trace gases. The nitrogen in the flue gas does not undergo transformation and returns into the
atmosphere. However, about 4.3% of the urea added to the makeup water is volatilized as NH3 gas (2%
of total nitrogen). Trace nutrients would need to be added but are considered negligible in both mass flow
and cost estimation. Evaporation of water accounts for typical annual pan evaporation numbers for the
NM region as well as increased loss from the flue gas sparging [8]. After the settler harvest, in which 95%
of the biomass is considered collected, 10% of the settler supernatant is sent to blowdown while the rest is
returned to the media reservoirs. Outside of the small surplus amount, the nutrients are in organic (bound
up in biomass) forms during the recycle and are not considered bioavailable. The centrifuge step also
assumes a 95% removal and concentration to 10% w/w solids, with the liquid centrate sent to blowdown.

3. ECONOMIC ANALYSIS
Capital (CAPEX) and operating (OPEX) expenses were developed based on the individual process area
designs, vendor quotes, industry references, and cost assumptions. These estimations were updated based
on mass balance results and were incorporated with a discounted cash flow rate of return (DCFROR)
analysis to determine the minimum biomass selling price (MBSP) to allow comparison between CRIAS
and industry standard at different production scales within MBE’s Environmental Sustainability and
Process Economics (ESPE) model. The 400-ha CRIAS scenario is used for reporting below.
3.1 Capital Cost Estimation
Raceways, storage ponds, and gravity settling ponds are designed and estimated by materials and labor
while other more complex unit processes, such as centrifuges, pumps, compressors, etc. are estimated
using cost curves. Some estimates are based on direct vendor quotes or past construction MBE has
participated in, while others are based on industry references such as RSMeans. The major capital cost
assumptions are listed in Table 3. All costs were adjusted to 2025 dollars using the construction cost
index in Appendix B.

Table 4 to determine total indirect cost (TIC). TDC and TIC are added to calculate the fixed capital
investment (FCI) of the facility, then a working capital factor of 5% is applied to calculate the TCI (Table
5).

The most significant contribution to capital expenses is the construction of the production ponds due to
the earthwork and the high material costs involved with installing pond liner. The cost of the flue gas
drying and compression is not portrayed in the capital costs, instead displayed as an annualized cost in the
variable operating expenses. This is for ease of computation, as a flue gas cost model was developed
separately from the facility cost model. However, the flue gas distribution capital expenses are accounted
for in Area 300.

3.2 Financing and Depreciation

A DCFROR financial analysis was performed in this study to estimate the worth of the dewatered
biomass, represented at MBSP. Due to level of variability in the TEA, a more detailed analysis does not
add value. Instead, conservative estimates were made to estimate reasonable cash flow from an nth plant
(NOAK) scenario.
Assumptions for the DCFROR analysis are in Table 6. MBSP is calculated in a goal-seek macro that
adjusts the sales price until the net present worth is zero, calculated by subtracting the TCI) + interest
across the 30 years from the summed annual present value (APV). The discount factor used to calculate
the APV for each year is calculated from the internal rate of return and year.
Interest payments, operating expenses (fixed and variable), annual sales and a general plant write-down
are factored in the net revenue. For tax purposes, losses are forwarded and income tax is applied to the
annual cash income. The IRS 45Q Tax credit is feasible to apply under the “Other Utilization Processes”
category, where the 12 kTCO2/yr use metric is met. Furthermore, photosynthetic fixation is an approved
utilization process according to Section 45Q(f)(5)(A) [9]. The high labor burden as well as the above-
prevailing wages rates used in the fixed expense calculation allows for the 5x multiplier as specified by
the prevailing wage and apprenticeship requirements [10]. This credit is only applicable for 12 years and
is modelled as such.

3.3 Operating Cost Estimation
The DCFROR analysis accounts for operating expenses as well, consisting of fixed and variable costs.
Essential assumptions for determining operating costs are shown in Table 7.

 Table 7 - Major operating costs assumptions

 Merchant CO2 $130 /tonne (see Table 1)
3.3.1 Fixed Operating Expenses
Fixed operating expenses include labor, overhead, depreciation, property insurance & taxes, and
maintenance. Labor rates were taken from the Bureau of Labor Statistics, using the mean annual wage for
New Mexico as the normalizing factor [13]. The New Mexico Department of Workforce Solutions
provided Public Works and Apprenticeship Application (PWAA) prevailing wage rates as well [10].
Some roles were considered part time (module operators) with full-time equivalent quantities scaled and
rounded up to the nearest whole number, while the rest were applied at full time. Equipment maintenance
at 2% of FCI was based on the higher end of the range for mature fossil fuel technology. The liner capital
cost was not included in the maintenance calculation since the liner should have little or no maintenance.
An additional labor burden of 75% is applied to the salary total, ~45% goes to bona fide fringe benefits (a
high fringe benefits rate), while the remaining 30% accounts for overhead expenses such as safety, plant
security, phone, light, heat, and plant communications.

3.3.2 Variable Operating Expenses
Variable operating costs are linked to the fluctuating nature of algal biology and regional climate patterns.
Unlike fixed costs, these expenses scale directly with biomass productivity, which shifts throughout the
seasonal cycle. The primary drivers of this variability include electrical consumption for cultivation and
processing and the delivery of nutrients through flue gas distribution and fertilizer addition (Figure 7).
The economic model accounts for a few distinct layers of variability that define the facility's financial
profile. Biological productivity remains a primary driver, as microalgae growth rates fluctuate in response
to shifting solar irradiance and ambient temperatures, causing the demand for CO2 and nutrient inputs to
scale accordingly. These biological shifts are compounded by mechanical requirements, like paddlewheel
drive efficiency and pond hydraulic profile. Furthermore, the harvesting frequency (as a function of
hydraulic residence time) affects water distribution electrical demand.

Flue gas variable operating costs are broken down in terms of electricity demand for compression and
drying, and annualized costs for the installation of the system based on a cost curve developed in prior
studies by MBE. Urea and diammonium phosphate costs are based on bulk purchase price by ton, while
all other costs are based on electricity.
3.4 Minimum Biomass Selling Price
The CAPEX and OPEX combined within the financial analysis detailed in Section 3.2 results in a
Minimum Biomass Selling Price (MBSP) for a tonne of algae ash-free dry weight, which is the
comprehensive metric used to compare all versions of the cultivation facility as it accounts not only for
CAPEX and OPEX but for depreciation, return on investment, income tax, and other details relevant to
commercial production of algae. According to industry sources, the plant-gate price for algae
biostimulants used for large-volume markets (e.g. field applications to crops) would be about $1,000 per
tonne. Therefore, the MBSP should be at or below that cost for the process to be commercially viable.
