 Figure 8 – MBSP for 40- and 400-ha facilities operating in the improved and baseline scenario.

Only the 400-ha improved CRIAS scenario met the $1,000 MBSP benchmark due to its improvements on
carbon utilization and productivity and large scale. Even with the improved process, the 40-ha scale is not
economically viable for this market, though smaller specialty markets for biostimulants in specialty crops,
or for smaller-scale applications (e.g. seed germination and development) would be feasible for even the
40-ha baseline case.
With an annual production of about 500 tonnes of biomass for the demonstration plant, this would
produce 5 million liters of algal biostimulants (at 10% concentration), or less than $1.25/acre, assuming
one gallon per acre application and a 200% marketing/distribution/profit mark-up. The market for
biostimulants is quite large and could support multiple NOAK type full scale plants.
To note: the 400-ha improved scenario is the only version capable of utilizing the IRS 45Q credit, as the
baseline scenarios do not utilize carbon from a powerplant (or similar emitting process) and the 40-ha
scenario does not utilize enough carbon to qualify for the credit. Finally, while the capital costs associated
with the baseline scenario are smaller (merchant CO2 addition requires less infrastructure and no 5-km
pipeline), the purchase and delivery of merchant CO2 is far more expensive.

4. PARAMETER ANALYSIS
The effects of the model input parameters on the overall MBSP were analyzed through sensitivity and
uncertainty analysis.
4.1 Sensitivity Analysis
A sensitivity analysis provides a view of the effect of a single variable on the overall MBSP, represented
by a % change. Rather than using a flat range for the variables, the “worst” and “best” case input

variables for the 400-ha improved scenario were individually modelled for a more accurate accounting of
the change in selling price (Figure 9).

 Figure 9 – Tornado plot for MBSP sensitivity analysis.

4.2 Uncertainty Analysis
An uncertainty analysis provides a potential range for the overall MBSP, worst-case and best-case
scenarios modelled (Figure 10). This is a left-skewed distribution, the expected case is the median while
the average created from a repeated random sampling simulation (n = 100) is lower than the expected
case. The interquartile range appears to be $1,110 – 660, with the absolute worst-case scenario skewing
far right (Figure 11).

5. CONCLUSIONS
The techno-economic analysis demonstrates that achieving high carbon utilization efficiency and
productivity targets is fundamentally linked to the integration of high-alkalinity-pH operational strategies
requiring algal strains able to assimilate CO2 efficiently under such conditions. By leveraging high CUE
with high productivity, the modeled 400-hectare facility projects an MBSP of $879, well below the
$1,000 per tonne benchmark for commercial viability for a large-scale biostimulants market, capable of
serving millions of acres of crops. The use of dilute flue gas from a nearby biogas combustion source,
avoiding the high costs of merchant CO2, is a major factor in this analysis. Even a 40-hectare
demonstration scale with a higher value biostimulants markets ($30/gallon of processed algae slurry)
would create a commercially-feasible, even if somewhat limited prospect.
At the 400-ha scale, the CRIAS process would be eligible for the IRS 45Q tax credit, a benefit only
accessible due to the high volume of CO2 utilized. While biological productivity and flue gas distribution
costs remain the most sensitive variables in the model, the low-impact construction approach—using
partially lined raceways and low-energy gravity settling—reduces capital investment. Ultimately, the
CRIAS technology platform establishes an economically plausible pathway for larger-scale agricultural
biostimulants production. Future efforts should focus on long-term field validation of the improved
strains' robustness and value as biostimulants.

 iii

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This Life Cycle Analysis (LCA) is submitted to the United States Department of Energy (DOE) National
Energy Technology Laboratory (NETL) under award DE-FOA-0002654, titled "Cultivation-Ready
Improved Algae Strains (CRIAS) for Increased Carbon Utilization Efficiency". This analysis evaluates
the environmental performance, specifically the life cycle greenhouse gas (GHG) impacts, of a novel
microalgae cultivation system designed to utilize anthropogenic carbon dioxide (CO2) from biogas
combustion sources. The Principal Investigators (PIs) leading this initiative are Drs. Juergen Polle and
John Benemann (MicroBio Engineering Inc.), with Dr. Omar Holguin (New Mexico State University),
Dr. Shawn Starkenburg (Los Alamos National Laboratory), Dr. Aubrey Davis (Cal Poly San Luis
Obispo), and Mike Lamont and Luke Cizek (Heliae Development LLC) providing support. This report
adheres to the ISO 14040/14044 standards for comparative life cycle assessments intended for public
disclosure [1].

The CRIAS project represents a strategic effort to overcome the economic and environmental barriers
associated with large-scale algal biomass production. A primary technology objective addressed in this
analysis is for a low-cost microalgae cultivation technology integrating flue-gas CO2 from biogas
combustion with cultivation and engineering techniques to increase carbon utilization efficiency (CUE).
The project targets also included development of algal strains with higher productivity (through improved
photon conversion efficiency) and the production of algal biomass for use as agricultural biostimulants.
This is a high-value but still large-scale market able to absorb the production from a large, 400 ha (1,000
acre) cultivation facility, and providing an entry point that also supports regenerative agriculture. In this
LCA, the boundary is the production of a 10% by dry weight, concentrated algal biomass, which can be
used directly as a biostimulant or further processed for specific and remote applications.

Based on the present LCA, this project transitioned during its development from Technology Readiness
Level (TRL) 4 (component validation in a laboratory environment) to TRL 5 (component and/or process
validation in relevant environment- Beta prototype). The geographic location of the modelled facility
matches one of the test sites (New Mexico), while the temporal boundary is 2025.

The functional unit for the study is defined as 1 kg of ash-free dry weight (AFDW) algal biomass
produced at the plant gate at a 10% solid concentration. In commerce, but not included herein, the product
could be further processed by freezing, refrigeration, addition of biocides, pasteurization, drying,
hydrolysis, etc. Higher value products, e.g. pigments (pyocyanin, phycoerythrin) and polysaccharides,
etc., could be extracted from the biomass, prior to use as biostimulants.

The system boundary is defined as Cradle-to-Gate, encompassing all unit processes from raw material
(nutrients, water) and energy supply through to the production of the concentrated biomass paste. The
analysis uses the OpenLCA software platform (version 2.5) paired with the NETL CO2U openLCA LCI
Database, GREET 2025, and ecoinvent 3.12 datasets for data quality consistent with DOE standards [2 3].

 4

Proposed Product System Description
The proposed system models a high-efficiency raceway pond cultivation system fed by biogas
combustion flue gas (approximately, and as herein used, 10% CO2). The model explicitly accounts for the
energy associated with flue gas capture, supply, and transfer (blowers, piping, transfer station), pond
mixing (paddlewheels), nutrient supply (diammonium phosphate and urea, minor elements are assumed
present in the water source), and extensive water recycling. The TEA model is based on a "high CUE"
(Carbon Utilization Efficiency), utilizing high pH/alkalinity chemistry for a favorable
pCO2aqueous/pCO2air equilibrium, that minimizes outgassing losses from the ponds.

Comparison Product System Description
The comparison system models the same scale commercial algae cultivation facility using merchant CO2,
the current standard process in microalgae cultivation. This carries a higher embodied energy burden than
the herein presented biogas CO2 source, due to capture, liquefaction and transportation requirements of
merchant CO2 sources. This LCA assumes standard commercial algal strains without the specific CRIAS
productivity improvements and utilizes conventional pond liners, reflecting the current industrial baseline
[4]. Liner cost reductions are a main goal in future technology improvements.

Figure 1. CO2 fixation into algal biomass and emissions for the proposed (using biogas CO2 flue gas)
and conventional (using merchant CO2) algae cultivation facility (based on CO2 emissions calculated
 by their Global Warming Potential for consistency (TRACI 2.1, 100-yr)

 5

The preliminary Life Cycle Impact Assessment (LCIA) results indicate that the Proposed Product System
offers a substantial improvement vs. the Comparison Product System, in overall net CO2 utilization
efficiency (CUE: Figure 1, the net difference in above and below the zero line, expressed by CO2 Global
Warming Potential, GWP). This improved performance is driven by three primary factors:
Displacement of Merchant CO2: The utilization of diverted flue gas eliminates the energy-intensive
supply chain associated with capturing, purifying, and transporting merchant CO2.
Increased Productivity: A biomass productivity of 15 g/m²/day (vs. baseline 10 g/m²/day) is projected,
based on improved strains, amortizes the embodied carbon of the pond infrastructure and the operational
energy of mixing over a larger mass of product, reducing the carbon intensity per kilogram of biomass.
Increased CUE: The changes in pond chemistry and increased flue gas contact time resulted in more
biogenic CO2 accumulation and less off-gassing, with a modelled CUE of 75%.
While the comparison product system uses less electricity (see the “US Power Grid Mixer” in Figure 1),
the low CUE results in more off-gassing and a higher demand for CO2, which is further compounded by
the higher net emissions from merchant CO2.
Sensitivity analysis highlights that the results are most sensitive to the CO2 Utilization Efficiency (CUE)
parameter, as represented by “Input CO2” and “CO2 Off-gassing”, as biogenic CO2 remains normalized
to mass. A decrease in CUE necessitates a larger volume of flue gas to meet the carbon demands of the
algae, thereby increasing the parasitic energy load of the delivery blowers and diminishing the net
“Carbon Benefit”.
A Carbon Benefit is relevant to both atmospheric CO2 loads (with CO2e reflecting total greenhouse
gases) and primary energy inputs to the US economy, in particular electricity generation, largely still, and
for the foreseeable future, based on fossil sources. As grid electricity is increasingly needed for Data
Centers, a focus on net CO2 emissions, with GWP serving as a proxy. This analysis demonstrates the
potentially favorable impact of this technology in the current US economy.

 6

1. GOAL AND SCOPE
1.1 Study Goal
The primary purpose of this study is to quantify the potential environmental benefits of the Cultivation-
Ready Improved Algae Strains (CRIAS) technology suite. The utilization of waste streams for biomass
production presents a pathway for utilizing industrial emissions while simultaneously producing valuable
biocommodities. This LCA evaluates the integration of advanced Scenedesmus obliquus strains with
optimized pond chemistry and biogas flue gas utilization, resulting in an increased CO2 utilization
efficiency, and coincidentally, lower global warming potential (GWP), compared to current industry-
standard algae production methods.
This project can, based on the outcomes, be classified as TRL 5, a level before larger- scale pilot work (in
ponds greater than 3.4 m2). This study emphasizes the technical potential of the biological and
engineering innovations in a comprehensive LCA study that emphasizes CO2 utilization and net
emissions, including other GHG sources, achieved under optimized conditions. The modeling focuses on
establishing the process’s environmental benefits rather than a detailed consequential market analysis or
broad market penetration, although the potential for such scale is implicitly considered in the
accompanying Techno-Economic Analysis (TEA) for 40- to 400- hectare facilities.
This study is conducted to understand the impact of the CRIAS process on a life cycle basis compared to
a conventional process producing the same dewatered biomass. This serves to identify "hotspots" within
the cultivation and harvesting process—such as electricity consumption for paddlewheels, embodied
carbon in nutrients, or parasitic loads of gas transfer—where further research and development should be
prioritized to maximize environmental performance. The integration of the companion Techno- Economic
Analysis (TEA) with this LCA ensures that environmental improvements are balanced against economic
viability.
This LCA report is submitted to the US DOE NETL, project managers, independent reviewers, and
partner /subcontractor organizations (New Mexico State University, Los Alamos National Laboratory,
Cal Poly, Heliae) for review and feedback to help guide ongoing R&D efforts and inform strategic
decision-making regarding future scale-up.
Public Disclosure
The LCAs conducted as part of the U.S. DOE Funding Opportunity Announcement (FOA) will become
part of the public record for the award within the final scientific/technical report.Study Scope
The scope of this LCA is defined to encompass the production of high-value algal biomass suitable for
use as an agricultural biostimulant. The study uses a Cradle-to-Gate approach, assessing impacts from raw
material acquisition through to the production of concentrated algal biomass at the facility gate. This
scope allows for a focused evaluation of the cultivation and harvesting innovations, independent of the
variability associated with final product formulation and distribution.

 7

1.2 STUDY SCOPE
The scope of this LCA encompasses the production of high-value algal biomass suitable for use as an
agricultural biostimulant. The study uses a Cradle-to-Gate approach, assessing impacts from raw material
acquisition through to the production of concentrated algal biomass at the facility gate. This scope allows
for a focused evaluation of the cultivation and harvesting innovations, independent of the final product
formulation, distribution and applications
1.2.1 Functional Unit of the Study
The functional unit serves as the reference basis for normalizing all inputs and outputs within the system.
For this study, the functional unit is defined as:
 1 kg of ash-free dry weight (AFDW) algae biomass, produced at 10% solids concentration at the
 plant gate.
This functional unit was selected because the primary output of the CRIAS process is biomass. While the
ultimate application may vary (e.g., biostimulants, biofuels, animal feed), the production of the biomass
itself is the primary energy and carbon-intensive step where the proposed innovations—specifically CUE
improvement and strain productivity—occur. Normalizing to AFDW accounts for variations in the ash
content of the algae, which can fluctuate based on environmental conditions and harvesting methods,
thereby providing a consistent biological basis for comparison. The 10% solids concentration represents a
standard output for primary dewatering technologies as well as an acceptable water content for conversion
into biostimulants, as specified by Heliae.
Multiproduct Functional Unit Considerations
The system includes a co-product interaction regarding the electricity generated by a biogas power plant
supplying the CO2. In accordance with NETL guidance, the system boundary includes the power plant as
the CO2 source. Therefore, the functional unit technically comprises the biomass product and the
electricity co-product allocated or displaced. However, for clarity in comparison and to align with the
primary research goal of biomass production, the results are normalized to the mass of algae produced,
with electricity impacts handled through system expansion. Note that in this study, there is no further
integration of the biogas power plant with the algae production process, such as utilization of digester
effluents in the algae production process.
1.2.2 System Boundary
The system boundary determines which unit processes are included in the LCA and separates the
technosphere from the ecosphere. This study utilizes a Cradle-to-Gate boundary, encapsulating all
processes necessary to create the product up to the point it leaves the production facility (Figure 2) [5].

 Figure 2. System Boundaries of the proposed (left) and comparison (right) product systems

 8

Excluded Processes
 2. Downstream Processing: Processes such as drying, extraction, cell disruption, or formulation into
 final biostimulant products are excluded. These steps occur after the "gate" and are assumed to be
 identical for both the proposed and comparison systems in terms of functional equivalence for
 biomass production, thus not affecting the comparative delta.

  3. Use Phase: The application of the biostimulant to crops and the subsequent end- of-life of the
 biomass (decomposition or consumption) are excluded.

  4. Capital Equipment Construction: While capital costs are rigorously tracked in the parallel Techno-
 Economic Analysis (TEA), the embodied emissions of building the facility infrastructure (e.g.,
 concrete for sumps, steel for paddlewheels) are generally excluded in this screening-level LCA unless
 they differ significantly between systems (e.g., the difference in pond liner requirements).

1.2.3 Carbon Dioxide Source
Proposed Product System
The source of carbon dioxide is a critical parameter in this CO2U study, distinguishing the proposed
innovation from standard practice. For the purposes of modelling, one of the NETL CO2 sources (Natural
Gas Combined Cycle Power Plant, no CO2 capture, cradle- to-gate, flue gas utilization) is used to
represent the CO2 derived from biogas-fired powerplant. In a following iteration, as the project advances
in TRL, a specific reference for a biogas-fired flue gas would be used with compositional data from the
source.
Comparison Product System
The comparison system utilizes merchant CO2. This represents the current standard commercial practice
for high-productivity algae farming (e.g., established facilities like Cyanotech and Earthrise) [6].
Merchant CO2 is modeled as a purchased industrial product, carrying with it the embodied life cycle
emissions associated with its capture (often from ammonia plants or geologic sources), purification,
liquefaction, and transportation to the algae facility. This flow is pulled from the ecoinvent 3.12 database
for “carbon dioxide production” under the “basic chemical manufacturing” subset.

1.2.4 Technology Representativeness
Proposed Product System
The technology represented in the TEA is an "Nth-of-a-kind" commercial implementation of the CRIAS
technology. It assumes the successful development and deployment of the Scenedesmus obliquus strains
(specifically derivatives of UTEX 3031) capable of achieving an annual average productivity of 15
g/m²/day and a Carbon Utilization Efficiency (CUE) of 75%. This represents a TRL projection from the
current experimental state (TRL 3-4) to a mature commercial state (TRL 9), assuming that the biological
innovations validated in the lab and outdoor pilot scale translate effectively to large-scale outdoor
environments. However, the project is still TRL 4 so market consequences were not considered in this
LCA.

Comparison Product System
The comparison product system represents the current state of the industry for algae cultivation. It
assumes productivity levels typical of standard commercial strains (approximately 10 g/m²/day) and
standard CUE (approximately 50% or lower). It utilizes conventional pond management techniques
without the advanced pH/alkalinity optimization proposed in the CRIAS project. This baseline provides a
realistic benchmark against which the benefits of the CRIAS innovations can be measured.

 9

1.2.5 Geographical Representativeness
The geographical scope of the study is the United States, specifically modeled for regions characterized
by high solar insolation suitable for year-round or extended- season algae cultivation. The primary focus
regions are the American Southwest, specifically New Mexico, aligning with on of the project's testbed
facilities in Las Cruces, NM. Consequently, grid electricity mixes, evaporation rates, and ambient
temperature data are selected to represent the WECC (Western Electricity Coordinating Council) region
to ensure the environmental impacts of energy and water consumption are accurately reflected [5].

1.2.6 Temporal Representativeness
The temporal boundary for the study is set for the period 2025-2030. This timeframe reflects the
anticipated deployment window for the technology following the conclusion of the 30-month project
timeline. Background datasets, such as ecoinvent and GREET, are selected to be the most recent available
versions (e.g., ecoinvent 3.12, GREET 2025) to ensure that the background energy and material profiles
are consistent with current and near-future industrial practices. It should be noted that the carbon “fixed”
to the algae biomass is not sequestered, but in the cradle-to-gate boundary it still serves as an offset
emission as it is still bound up in the biomass.

1.2.7 Life Cycle Impact Assessment Methods for Results Interpretation
This study utilizes the TRACI 2.1 (Tool for Reduction and Assessment of Chemicals and Other
Environmental Impacts) method for impact assessment [7].

GHG Categories
  • Global Warming Potential (GWP-100): Calculated using IPCC AR5 factors for a 100-year time
 horizon (kg CO2eq). This metric includes carbon-climate feedback mechanisms to provide a
 comprehensive assessment of the climate impact [8]. It should be noted that this impact can also
 be a stand-in or proxy for the benefits of CO2 utilization in such processes, independent of effects
 on global warming.

Other Categories Reported
 • Acidification Potential (AP): Measured in kg SO2eq. This category is relevant due to the
 potential for ammonia volatilization from the ponds, especially at the high pH levels proposed in
 the CRIAS system.
 • Eutrophication Potential (EP): Measured in kg Neq. This is critical given the nutrient-intensive
 nature of algae cultivation and possible use of reclaimed wastewater resources.
 • Water Consumption (WC): Measured in kg water. Vital for open pond cultivation systems
 located in arid regions where evaporation rates are high.
 • Ozone Depletion Potential (ODP): Measured in kg CFC-11eq. No strong relevance for this
 project.
 • Particulate Matter Formation Potential (PMFP): Measured in kg PM2.5eq. Relevant in the case of
 electricity production.
 • Photochemical Smog Formation Potential (PSFP): Measured in kg O3eq. Relevant in the case
 of flue gas use.

1.2.8 Completeness Requirements
The study aims for a high level of completeness to ensure the validity of the comparative assertion. All
material and energy flows contributing more than 1% to the cumulative mass or energy input of the
system are included in the model. A carbon mass balance was performed to account for at least 99% of
the carbon inputs and outputs (biomass, outgassing, loss to blowdown). This ensures that the carbon
utilization efficiency claims are substantiated by a closed mass balance (Table 1).

1.2.9 Sensitivity and Uncertainty Analysis
A "one-at-a-time" sensitivity analysis will be conducted on key parameters to determine their individual
influence on the final GWP result. Key parameters identified for this analysis include:
 • Carbon Utilization Efficiency (CUE), represented by CO2 input and CO2 off- gassing
 • Nutrient Input, represented by urea and DAP
 • Electricity, represented by total grid power mix
Each parameter was decreased by 10% for the sensitivity check, with the results recorded in Figure 3
(Section 2.5).
Uncertainty Analysis
Uncertainty will be addressed by defining "Low” (best-case), "Expected," and "High" (worst-case)
scenarios for the key parameters listed above. This bracketing approach allows for the quantification of
the range of potential environmental outcomes and assesses the robustness of the claim that the CRIAS
system is superior to the baseline under varying operational conditions. Unlike the sensitivity analysis, the
uncertainty analysis was performed by updating the ESPE mass balance with the same ranges as detailed
in the TEA (Table 2).

2. LIFE CYCLE INVENTORY ANALYSIS
2.1 Modeling Platform
The Life Cycle Inventory (LCI) model for this project was constructed using OpenLCA (Version 2.5.0),
an open-source LCA software package.
Supporting the OpenLCA model is the Environmental Sustainability and Process Economics (ESPE)
model, an Excel spreadsheet developed by MicroBio Engineering Inc. (MBE). It calculates the
fundamental mass and energy balances—such as the stoichiometric CO2 demand based on biomass
composition, the hydraulic power requirements for paddlewheels, and evaporative water losses—which
are then imported as static parameters or flow quantities into the OpenLCA framework. This hybrid
approach ensures that the LCA is grounded in rigorous engineering calculations.

A simplified version of the system boundaries with major inputs and outputs highlighted is shown in

Figure 3. Proposed product system simplified flow diagram

Recycled carbon and process/media water is contained within the block and not shown. Also not pictured
is the dewatered biomass paste produced from the system, but the red-highlighted CO2 is the converted
carbon bound up within the biomass, a negative emission.
2.3 Data Sources and Quality Assessment
Data quality is prioritized by using empirical data from the project's testbeds whenever possible to ensure
the model reflects the specific innovations of the CRIAS project.
Primary Data: Productivity rates, CUE values, and pond chemistry requirements are derived directly
from experimental data generated at the Las Cruces, NM, and San Luis Obispo, CA testbeds during
Budget Periods 1 and 2. This includes data on the CUE and productivity performance of the S. obliquus
strains.
Secondary Data: Background processes for nutrient production (urea, DAP), electricity grid mixes, and
transport logistics are sourced from the NETL CO2U openLCA Database, GREET 2025, and ecoinvent
v3.12. These datasets are widely recognized standards in LCA.
Geography: Primary data is site-specific (NM), reflecting the high-insolation environments targeted for
deployment. Secondary data is US-average or WECC regional mix where appropriate, except for the
Ecoinvent merchant CO2 which is “Rest- of-World.”
Time: Primary data is current (2024-2026). Secondary data is the most recent available in the databases
(typically 2016-2023), ensuring temporal relevance.
2.4 Results of Inventory Completeness Check
A carbon mass balance was performed on the OpenLCA model outputs to validate the system's integrity
and is included in this submission. The balance accounts for carbon in the biomass, carbon lost to
outgassing (calculated via pH/alkalinity models), carbon dissolved in blowdown water, and carbon
recycled in harvest water. The model successfully closes the carbon balance to within 99%, satisfying the
NETL completeness requirement and validating the CUE calculations.
2.5 Results of Life Cycle Inventory Model Sensitivity Check
The inventory model was tested by decreasing the parameters listed in Section 1.2.9 by 10% to assess the
sensitivity of the results (Figure 4).

3. LIFE CYCLE IMPACT ASSESSMENT
3.1 Life Cycle Impact Assessment Methods
The TRACI 2.1 method is used for all impact categories, ensuring consistency with U.S. EPA and NETL
standards. The Global Warming Potential (GWP) is calculated using IPCC AR5 factors for a 100-year
time horizon (GWP100)
 • CO2: 1 kg CO2eq
 • CH4: 36 kg CO2eq (inclusive of climate-carbon feedback)
    • N2O: 298 kg CO2eq
Non-GWP impact assessment categories also included in the report are listed in Section 1.2.7.
3.2 Data Quality Assessment
Key components of proposed system processes (CUE, productivity, nutrient quantities) are based on
pilot-scale results but are not collected from year-round data and therefore had to be adjusted to meet
realistic year-round expectations, which reduces the quality of the primary data. Other processes and
flows were based on well- documented inventory information from NETL, GREET, and Ecoinvent.
One significant detriment to data quality was the lack of a full biogas combustion flue gas inventory
analysis. There was limited empirical data from the CRIAS project and while biogas processes are
included in some databases, NGCC flue gas was considered to be equivalent to a similar turbine-driven
biogas power plant and is an acceptable NETL CO2 flow.

4. LIFE CYCLE INTERPRETATION
The results of this LCA indicate that the CRIAS technology significantly outperforms standard algae
cultivation in terms of Global Warming Potential (Error! Reference source not found.).

 -2.5 Net GHG Results
 CRIAS Proposed 400ha Industry Standard
 Comparison 400ha
 Figure 5. Global Warming Potential of the proposed product system and the comparison industry
 standard system.

The error bars shown represent the worst- and best-case scenarios for the proposed case, as well as
different forms of merchant CO2 production (ammonia plant, or refinery capture and processing) for the
comparison case. The best-case scenario for the industry standard comparison uses the same parameters
as the expected proposed case, but uses merchant CO2 (as per ecoinvent, see section 1.2.3) as the CO2
source.

The worst-case version runs using ammonia plant CO2 production.

Even the worst-case scenario for the proposed product system results in ~0.1 kg CO2eq less than the best-
case comparison product system scenario that models the improvements in CUE and productivity.

The primary drivers for this improvement are:

 17

 1. Elimination of Merchant CO2 Impacts: Diverting flue gas directly removes the energy-intensive
 processes of capturing, liquefying, and transporting merchant CO2, which is a major carbon
 hotspot in conventional cultivation.

 2. Productivity Gains: The increase from ~10g/m²/day (standard) to 15 g/m²/day (CRIAS) means
 that the fixed environmental "overhead" of the pond construction and operation (specifically
 paddlewheel mixing energy) is amortized over more than double the product yield, drastically
 improving efficiency.

 3. High CUE: The 75% CUE target ensures that the energy spent moving flue gas is efficiently
 converted into biomass, minimizing the "parasitic" carbon penalty of the blower systems.
 Furthermore, unused CO2 escapes into the environment, increasing off-gassing emissions. The
 50% CUE and merchant CO2 in the comparison case results in a large amount of off-gassed CO2
 escaping into the atmosphere.

The sensitivity analysis performed confirms that CUE is the most impactful parameter, measured in
delivered CO2 and off-gassed CO2. There is a breakeven point if the CUE drops to below 40% in the
proposed system while the comparison system maintains a CUE above 60% and uses ammonia
production-derived CO2 as its source. However, this proposed system has a clear reduction in emissions
compared to the industry standard and could be a promising new technique if the TRL can be increased
towards scale-up.

Data Limitations
sustained annually. If real-world conditions (weather variability, pest contamination) limit productivity to
10 - 12 g/m²/day, the environmental benefits would be reduced, though likely still superior to the baseline.
The estimated ~40-day downtime is also optimistic for algae production, which can be interrupted by
crashes or other cultivation failures. However, the high pH maintained for the high CUE may provide an
extra degree of protection against pests or competing algae for the strain of Scenedesmus sp. that is
accustomed to the conditions.
Additionally, the study relies on generic background data for nutrient production, which may not capture
regional variations in fertilizer manufacturing efficiency. The emissions are also exceedingly low as there
is no truck/rail transport of dewatered biomass or any downstream processing. A cradle-to-grave LCA of
a biostimulant would result in higher emissions due to the release of carbon in the biomass as it
decomposes. However, it would also allow displacement of conventional fertilizers/soil amendments
which would offset these increases.
Conclusion
The CRIAS project demonstrates a viable pathway to low-carbon algal biomass production. The
integration of biology (improved S. obliquus strains) and engineering (pond chemistry, flue gas contact
time) creates a synergistic effect that lowers the carbon intensity of the final product significantly below
that of the comparison system. The results support the continued development of this technology as a key
component of the DOE's carbon utilization strategy.

5. CRITICAL REVIEW
The U.S. DOE Carbon Utilization Program will serve as the critical reviewer for this study.
